# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project uses [maintenance-based versioning](TOKENSAVE-VERSIONING.md), not SemVer.

## [Unreleased]

### Changed
- **A sync no longer loads every unresolved reference in the graph at once (#482).** It materialised the whole `unresolved_refs` table on every sync, including a one-line edit: 189,446 records and **+74.6 MiB** on tokensave's own tree, the second largest allocation a sync makes. #409 concluded the resolution pass could not be chunked, and for the *nodes* that is right — binding a cross-file reference means looking up a name that may be defined anywhere, so a chunked name index silently loses targets and drops edges, which is a correctness regression dressed as a memory win. The references are the other input and have no such property: `resolve_batch` is a `par_iter().map(resolve_one)`, so every reference is answered against the whole index independently. The index stays global; only the input is paged, 25,000 at a time, with a keyset cursor on the table's autoincrement `id` rather than `LIMIT`/`OFFSET` so paging the table stays linear instead of quadratic in the number of pages. What still accumulates is bounded by the *answers* rather than the questions — 28,849 resolved and 11,713 ambiguity records against 189,446 inputs — and the one step that cannot be done per page, suppressing the bare-name sibling of a resolved Go selector call (#153 Bug 1), runs once over the accumulated set. **Verified equivalent rather than assumed:** indexing this repository with and without the change produces byte-identical results across all 27,897 edges and every ambiguity record, and the tests compare one-batch against many-batch at page sizes down to a single reference per page. Measured peak RSS on this repository, five runs, one added comment line, CLI `sync`: **215.8 → 175.5 MiB (median)**, and **247.0 → 175.5 MiB against the start of this line of work** — a 29% reduction, none of it from the two redesigns #409 proposed.
- **`resolve_all` no longer clones every reference that fails to resolve (#483).** It built a `Vec<UnresolvedRef>` of the failures — the name pre-filter's rejects wholesale, plus a clone for every candidate that failed or fell below the confidence floor. On tokensave's own tree that is roughly **160,000 owned records per sync**, several `String`s each, because 189,446 references go in and 28,849 resolve. They bought nothing: the caller already owns the slice it passed in, so an index is the same information, and the two uses were an iteration that can borrow (deriving the ambiguity records) and a returned field that **nothing in the product reads** — its only consumers are two assertions in `tests/resolution_test.rs`, which is why it went unnoticed. `ResolutionResult.unresolved` is now `Vec<u32>` of positions in the input slice, and the failures are carried borrowed. Measured on this repository, five runs, one added comment line, CLI `sync`: **peak RSS 238.1 → 215.8 MiB (median)**. One incidental improvement: the field is now in input order, where before it listed the pre-filter's rejects first and the resolution failures second, so its order depended on the partition rather than on the caller's slice.
- **Memory phase samples are now taken after the work they name, and the unresolved-ref load has one of its own (#409).** Every sample in the resolution block was emitted *before* the step it was named for, so each one reported the RSS of the *previous* step under the next step's label. That is not a cosmetic slip: it is why the whole-graph node load was believed to cost ~73 MiB and became the entire subject of #409's four proposed redesigns. Corrected, on this repository with one added comment line, the node load costs **16.2 MiB** — the 73 belonged to loading the 189,446 unresolved references, which had no sample at all and appears in none of that issue's options. The corrected ranking is: load unresolved refs +74.6 MiB, `resolve_all` +97.7 MiB, load the node slice +16.2 MiB, build the resolver caches +6.4 MiB. The two the issue was about are the two smallest, together 10% of the sync's growth. `sync:resolve:load_refs` is new; `load_nodes` and `build_caches` now mean what they say.
- **The build-variant pass no longer reads the whole graph, and the sync's true peak is finally visible (#481, groundwork for #409).** `propagate_variant_edges` loaded every `annotates` and `calls` edge — and did it while the resolver's node slice was still alive, so two graph-sized allocations were resident at once. On this repository that pass held the *entire sync's* peak RSS and emitted **zero** edges. It was doing a whole-graph pass to find a set that is tiny by construction: @vianbas replayed the grouping rule in SQL and found 237 `cfg`-annotated nodes, 82 callable and in a Rust file, forming 79 groups of which **3 have two members**, with exactly 4 `calls` edges pointing at any member — so 4 was the ceiling, and a measured run hit 0. The output set being provably small is the whole argument for asking SQL rather than Rust: two bounded queries now find the variant groups and then only the `calls` edges targeting a member, and the common case — no multi-member group — returns before touching the edges table at all. Both feed the same emitter the whole-graph path uses, split out so the two cannot drift, and an equivalence test runs a real indexed graph through both. That test withdraws an already-propagated edge before comparing, because the steady state after indexing has both twins' edges already and "same answer" would otherwise be two empty vectors proving nothing; it asserts the whole-graph pass found something before asserting the two agree. The Go query pre-filters on a *repeated name* rather than on directory, since SQLite has no `dirname` — a superset of the real groups, bounded by duplicate names instead of by graph size, with the exact `(directory, name)` grouping done in Rust. The `cfg` gate is kept as a correctness condition rather than an optimisation: without it two impls that merely share a `qualified_name` (`From<A>`/`From<B>`, both named `from`) would be fused, and a test asserts they are not. Measured on this repository, one added comment line, CLI `sync`: **peak RSS 247.0 → 221.4 MiB**. The full-index path is untouched — there the nodes and edges are already in memory, so it pays no extra load. Reported by @vianbas.
- **Two phase samples added, because the sync's peak was invisible to its own telemetry (#409).** `tokensave memory` records the phase of the highest *sample*, and there was no sample between `sync:resolve:done` and `sync:done` — so the peak, which lives in that window, was attributed to whichever sample came next. `tokensave memory` duly reported `sync:done`, ~27 MiB below the real high-water mark and two phases late. That blind spot is why #409 has been argued from `size_of` arithmetic rather than from RSS, and why the largest single consumer went unnoticed: `resolve_all` is **+97 MiB** on a one-line sync, more than the node load and the caches combined, and neither of that issue's proposed options touches it. `sync:resolve:refs` and `index:resolve:refs` now bracket the resolution work, and `sync:variants` the build-variant pass. The peak on this repository now reports as `sync:resolve:refs` rather than `sync:done`, which is both correct and the point.


### Fixed

- **Oh My Pi lifecycle operations now keep global profile ownership consistent when the active profile changes.** Global install records each resolved OMP agent directory before updating its MCP and rules surfaces, and reinstall refreshes every still-existing recorded profile instead of only the profile active at that moment. Migration detection and doctor inspect active, recorded, and legacy default profiles, while uninstall removes Tokensave from all known profiles without creating a newly selected profile and retains failed targets for a later retry. Resolver output and the versioned profile registry are validated before mutation, registry updates are locked and atomic, and `primary_config_path` reports only the currently resolved active profile.
- **The grep guardrail no longer denies a whole command because the extra work is a substitution or a redirect (#480).** #475 established that a search is only ever a *suggestion* to ask the graph instead, so it must never veto a line it does not fully model — a denial returns the entire command unrun, and destroying a `./deploy.sh` to save a grep is the worse failure. `has_chained_command` implemented that rule for `&&`, `||`, `;`, `|` and a backgrounding `&`, but those are not the only ways a line carries work the hook cannot serve. A command substitution runs a command of its own, and ``grep -n my_function src/lib.rs `date +%s` `` or `grep -n "$(cat patterns.txt)" src/lib.rs` was denied outright, so the substitution never ran; an output redirect is a file the caller asked for, and `grep -n my_function src/lib.rs > /tmp/hits` produced no file. Both now pass through untouched, in double-quoted regions as well as bare, since `$(…)` and backticks expand inside double quotes. `2>`/`2>>` is deliberately *not* included: routing the search's own stderr away discards nothing the caller depends on, so the line still is just the search, the graph is a real substitute for it, and the suggestion stands. The same rule governs the `find`/`fd` discovery path, which shares the scanner. Reported by @dangeReis.

### Fixed

- **`githooks on` no longer exits 0 after failing to install a hook (#488).** Both paths printed a red `✘ Failed to open … for writing` and then returned success, so a setup script gating on the exit code carried on with no hooks installed and no way to tell. The global path called `write_global_hook`, which returns a `bool`, in an `if` whose `else` did not exist; the local path collected `installed` and `already_present` but had nowhere to put a hook that failed. `offer_git_post_commit_hook` now returns `Result<(), String>` and `LocalHookInstall` carries a `failed` list, which `githooks on` turns into a non-zero exit naming the hooks. Failures are collected rather than raised at the first one: the command installs three hooks, and bailing early would skip the two the user also asked for. Declining the prompt, an already-present hook, and the best-effort call inside `install` all stay success — only an explicit `githooks on` that could not do what it was asked now fails.

## [7.11.0] - 2026-08-30

### Added
- **Per-repository git hooks, so keeping the index fresh no longer costs a machine-wide setting (#455).** tokensave's git hooks worked one way: claim `core.hooksPath`. That is a **single global setting**, and it overrides git's default of a separate `.git/hooks` per checkout — so accepting the hooks forced one hook directory on every repository on the machine, which is the wrong answer for anyone whose projects need different tooling. `tokensave githooks on --local` installs the same three hooks (`post-commit`, `post-checkout`, `post-merge`) into the repository's own hook directory and **writes no git config at all**, so no other repository is affected; `--local` works on `off` and the bare status form too, and `--path` targets a repository other than the working directory. `tokensave init` now offers them, defaulting to yes on a terminal and staying silent otherwise, with `--git-hook` and `--no-git-hook` to decide up front — local rather than global because `init` is per-project by definition. The offer is skipped when tokensave's global hooks are already installed, since a repository covered by both would run a sync twice per commit. Four details worth knowing. Writing is additive in the same way the global path is, so a repository already running husky, pre-commit, or a hand-written `post-commit` keeps everything it had and gains a marked tokensave section, and removal keeps any content tokensave did not write while deleting a file that is nothing but ours. There is deliberately **no chaining preamble** on local hooks: the global ones forward to `.git/hooks` because claiming `core.hooksPath` would otherwise disable it, but a local hook *is* that file and a forwarder would invoke itself. The hook directory is resolved from `--git-common-dir` rather than `--git-dir`, because linked worktrees share one hook directory with the main checkout and resolving the other way would write to a per-worktree directory git never reads. And if a `core.hooksPath` is in effect for the repository from any config scope, git resolves every hook from there with no fallback, so the hooks would be written and never run — that is reported rather than passed off as a successful install, which is the reporter's own complaint pointed the other way. The first half of the report, that `tokensave install` should not modify global hooks by default, was already the behaviour: the prompt defaults to no and a non-TTY skips it entirely. Reported by @jmagnusson.
- **`tokensave serve --idle-timeout-secs <N>`, an opt-in deadline for hosts that leak servers (#436).** Some MCP hosts start a **new** server per subagent and keep every one alive after the subagent that used it has finished. Every retained server is a direct child of the same *still-live* supervisor, so nothing ever closes its stdin and the EOF that would normally stop it never arrives — and a parent-PID watchdog cannot help, because the parent is not dead. A controlled two-subagent pilot on Windows added two servers that both persisted, with four totalling roughly 113 MB private memory, 266 MB working set and 922 handles, each holding its index open. The reporter's analysis was that this is an upstream host defect (openai/codex#38754) and that tokensave's own teardown path is correct, and both halves stand — the same supervisor retained duplicate fleets of unrelated MCP servers after tokensave was removed from its config. What tokensave can do is stop waiting. With the flag set, a server that goes `N` seconds without a request exits through the existing graceful, idempotent shutdown; omitting it preserves today's indefinite lifetime exactly, so no existing setup changes. Three properties make it safe to leave on. The deadline is evaluated **only** while the loop is parked waiting for the next request — the timer is created fresh at each park — so it can never interrupt a request, and because requests are handled serially no in-flight counter is needed. It measures *idleness rather than uptime*: each request restarts the window, so a server busier than its timeout never expires. And an expiry landing while a detached startup job is still running — the startup catch-up sync, or a version-bump reindex — is deferred and waits out another whole window, so the option cannot abandon work the next request depends on. The deferral rule is deliberately asymmetric and pinned by its truth table: catch-up is spawned unconditionally so "not done" really means "still running", while a reindex defers only while *started and not finished*, since keying on `!done` alone would make every ordinary session defer forever and the option silently do nothing. **It stays off by default** because enabling it depends on the host starting a fresh server when a tool is called after an idle exit, which varies by host and is not something tokensave can probe. It is host-integration policy rather than project state, so it lives on the command line only: there is no config-file equivalent and `tokensave install` does not write it into generated host config. Related: the reapability half of this issue — a server that ignored `SIGTERM` and needed `SIGKILL` — shipped separately in `1a9b983`, and #421's registry makes the retained servers enumerable in the first place. Reported by @jasonb194.
- **`tokensave servers` names which running server holds which index (#421).** `serve` keeps an exclusive handle on its database for the life of the process — file watching is why the process is long-lived, and the handle comes with it — so an indexed directory cannot be deleted while a client has a server up. On Windows that fails outright rather than deferring, and it bites hardest on git worktrees: a worktree per task, each one indexed, each one cleaned up afterwards, with `git worktree remove` deregistering the worktree even when the file delete fails and leaving git metadata pruned against a directory still on disk. The lock itself was never the problem — the problem was that it had no owner you could name. The reporter's machine had seven servers running and **five carried no project in argv at all**, because an MCP host supplies the project through the global database or `initialize` roots rather than `--path`, so neither `ps` nor Task Manager can answer "which of these holds this index". This is a capability regression rather than a new defect: before v6.0.0 the long-lived process was the daemon, and the daemon had `--status` to print its PID; `serve` inherited none of that, so v6.0.0 replaced a partly-broken stop path with no path at all, and the workaround in the field became correlating the SQLite `-shm` sidecar's mtime against process start times — inference from an implementation detail, which fails outright when two servers start in the same second. Each server now writes `~/.tokensave/servers/<pid>.json` once its database is open, recording `pid`, `started_at`, `project_path`, `argv_path`, `db_path` and `version`; `tokensave servers [--json]` lists them. That makes both directions answerable from files: **index → process** by matching `db_path`, which is what deleting a stuck checkout needs, and **enumerate all** by listing the directory, which is what a wrapper needs to rebuild a list UI. Four deliberate choices. The registry lives in the *global* directory rather than beside the index, because a PID file inside `.tokensave/` would sit in the one directory whose defining problem is that it cannot be deleted, and would vanish with the checkout being cleaned up. `started_at` records the **process** start time as the OS reports it rather than the write time, which turns the reporter's fragile mtime correlation into a contract: a live process at a recorded PID whose start time disagrees is a different process, so PID reuse is detected instead of guessed. `db_path` is recorded separately from `project_path` because a per-branch database is not derivable from the project root, and matching on the wrong one would silently miss. And `argv_path` is kept alongside the resolved path so a reader can reconcile the registry against what a process lister shows. Entries are removed on clean exit, and reaped both on any `serve` startup and on every read, so a hard kill that skips the clean-exit path self-heals within one listing. **There is deliberately no `servers --stop`:** MCP clients restart their servers, so "stop them all" does not converge — new PIDs appear while old ones are being killed — and terminating is safe only for a caller who knows whether the host is running and can stop it first, which tokensave cannot know. Identification is the part tokensave owes; the kill stays with whoever has that context, and `serve` now honours a `SIGTERM` on that PID since #436/#450. Related to #437, which reports the server's own resolved root — that covers the diagnosis direction; this covers the reverse lookup. Reported by @xaerogonzo.
- **Obsidian Canvas (`.canvas`) boards are now indexed (#459).** A `.canvas` file is the [JSON Canvas](https://jsoncanvas.org) format Obsidian uses for node-graph boards, and it had no extractor, so every sync reported it as an unsupported extension and its contents never reached the graph. In a vault that uses Canvas for architecture maps or planning boards that is often where the *structure* lives — the relationships between notes rather than the note bodies — so the practical symptom was that a term written only on a canvas was unfindable, and a note reachable only through canvas edges looked orphaned even though the vault links it. Because the format is plain JSON with a documented schema, this is a hand-rolled `serde_json` reader rather than a tree-sitter grammar, so it adds no grammar table to the binary. Each `type: "text"` card becomes a `Module` node — the same kind the Markdown extractor gives a heading, so a card and a note section behave alike in search — named from its first heading or first non-empty line and carrying the card's full Markdown as its docstring, which is what makes an arbitrary term on a card reachable. Each `type: "group"` with a label gets one too, since a group label is authored text like any other. Each `type: "file"` card emits a `Uses` edge to the note it points at, and entries in `edges[]` become `Uses` edges between the cards they connect, so board structure is preserved. Two deliberate choices: the `file` target is **not** filtered to code extensions the way Markdown link handling is, because a canvas card almost always points at another `.md` note and those are precisely the edges that made a linked note look orphaned; and an edge whose endpoint is a `file` card is not emitted a second time, since that card's own reference already represents it. `type: "link"` cards are skipped, having only an external URL the graph cannot resolve. A canvas that is not valid JSON still yields its `File` node plus a reported error, so a malformed board stays visible to `tokensave_files` rather than vanishing the way an unsupported extension did. Gated behind the new `lang-canvas` feature, in the `full` tier. `.base` (Obsidian Bases) is not covered — the reporter rated it lower priority and the format is newer and less widely used — so it still appears in the skipped-extension line. Reported by @jasonb194.
- **`tokensave doctor` now reports an index whose *scope* is wrong, not just one whose contents are (#450).** A home directory initialized as a project — the shape #372 could produce — leaves an index that every later `serve` inherits and keeps syncing, and nothing surfaced it: `serve`'s guardrail refuses *uninitialized* directories, and this directory is initialized, just absurd in scope. The reported case reached 29.9 GB on disk and a 38 GB `phys_footprint` once mapped, against a real project on the same machine at 420 MB, with the machine paging heavily and the server still syncing the home tree at 50–70% CPU two and three-quarter hours in. A new `Index scope` section reports two things: whether `$HOME` itself is initialized as a project, checked regardless of the working directory since the whole problem with that state is that nobody is standing in it when it does the damage; and whether the current project's index has passed 5 GB, a line set well above any plausible monorepo because a false positive costs one line of output. The size counts the WAL as well as the database, which is where an actively-syncing runaway shows up first and is part of what a running server maps. Both are warnings rather than failures — neither state is corrupt, and the remedy is a judgment call — and a directory that is both `$HOME` and the working directory is reported once. This is the third of the reporter's three suggestions; the scope cap for `serve` and a SIGTERM handler that interrupts an in-flight sync are still open on the issue. Reported by @not-antoni.
- **First-class Oh My Pi (`--agent omp`) integration for global profiles and project-local installs.** Global install, uninstall, and doctor operations use a bounded bare `omp config path` lookup, so exported `OMP_PROFILE`/`PI_PROFILE` selections and OMP-owned `PI_CONFIG_DIR`/`PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` overrides target the profile OMP actually owns rather than a guessed Pi-compatible path; cheap detection and migration checks remain subprocess-free against the default profile. Local installs use `.omp/mcp.json` and `.omp/rules/tokensave.md` without requiring the OMP CLI. The installer preserves unrelated MCP servers and settings, refreshes an OMP-native `alwaysApply: true` advisory rule carrying an exact ownership marker, and uninstall removes only Tokensave-owned entries and rules. Doctor reports resolver failures plus missing, malformed, stale, or wrong-command surfaces. Hook enforcement is deliberately unsupported until Tokensave has a proven OMP-specific hook contract.
- **A global `post-merge` git hook now runs `tokensave sync` after `git pull`.** `tokensave install`'s git-hook offer previously wired only `post-commit` (after your own commits) and `post-checkout` (fresh clones and branch switches), so a plain `git pull` that fast-forwards or produces a merge commit fired neither — a teammate's changes landed on disk with no hook to pick them up, and the index stayed stale until your next local commit. `post-merge` is now a third owned hook, installed and removed alongside the other two under the same `--git-hook` opt-in, `tokensave githooks on`/`off`, and `tokensave uninstall`'s `--keep-git-hooks` guard; `tokensave githooks` (no action) reports it as owned like the others. It carries its own idempotency marker so an existing install migrates the next time `tokensave install` runs, and it chains to a repository's own `post-merge` hook (e.g. a Husky-managed one) the same way `post-commit`/`post-checkout` already do, so claiming a global `core.hooksPath` never silently disables a repo's own pull hook.
- **Ruby `define_method`/`define_singleton_method` with a statically-resolvable name now emit a first-class method node.** Previously these DSL definitions produced no node at all, making them invisible to `search`, `module_api`, `callers`, `rename_preview`, `dead_code` and `doc_coverage`, and leaving every call to them permanently unresolvable. The new method gets the same kind, visibility, `Contains` edge, complexity metrics and outgoing calls an equivalent `def` would.
- **Ruby `module_function` now emits both the private instance method and the public singleton copy it actually defines.** Previously the extractor emitted a single public instance method, so `ERB::Util.html_escape_once`-style constant-receiver calls into a `module_function` method could never resolve and the method showed up as public API when it wasn't. The bare mode switch, `module_function :foo, :bar`, and inline `module_function def foo; end` are all handled, with the singleton copy carrying the same docstring, calls, and complexity as the `def` it mirrors.
- **`tokensave githooks`, and git-hook removal as part of `tokensave uninstall` (#420).** Installing offered global `post-commit` and `post-checkout` hooks; nothing removed them. Since the hooks are global and outlive every agent integration, uninstalling from all agents and deleting a project's `.tokensave/` still left a hook that recreated the index on the next commit — the reporter's exact sequence. `tokensave githooks` now shows which hooks tokensave owns and where, `tokensave githooks off` removes them, `tokensave githooks on` installs them without a prompt, and a global `tokensave uninstall` removes them too unless `--keep-git-hooks` is passed. `--local` never touches them, since the hooks are global rather than project-scoped. Removal is conservative in three ways: a hook file holding anything the user wrote keeps that content and loses only tokensave's marked section; a file that is nothing but tokensave's own content is deleted; and `core.hooksPath` is unset — with the directory removed — only when it points at tokensave's own default, that directory is left empty, and the run actually removed a tokensave hook, so a `core.hooksPath` the user set themselves is never edited by a command that found nothing of ours. The section boundary handles all three shapes that have shipped, including the unfenced `post-checkout` bodies from 6.4.3 and 7.3.0 that end in a bare `fi`. Reported by @jasonb194.

### Changed
- **Installed agent rules text is now one canonical body with per-harness rendering and versioned, refreshable blocks (#441).** `tokensave install` used to copy a stale, per-agent rules paragraph into global instruction files and never refresh it in shared owner-edited files because the marker was a plain heading append. The canonical source now lives in `src/agents/rules.rs` and covers `tokensave_status`, `graph_root` for cross-project queries, branch-specific tools for cross-branch queries, scoping MCP use to code tasks, finding the active database in `.tokensave/branch-meta.json` before any SQL fallback, and guarding read-only tasks from writing the index. Claude Code still gets its mandatory "no Explore agents" overlay on the same core, Kiro keeps its delegate-tool rule as a similar overlay, and the remaining integrations (Gemini, Grok, Kimi, Qwen, Vibe) now render the same canonical body, so all thirteen installed harnesses share one rules source with doctor coverage. Shared files (`AGENTS.md`, `copilot-instructions.md`, and Pi's `~/.agents/AGENTS.md`) use HTML-comment markers with a content hash so `install` and `reinstall` refresh the tokensave block in place while preserving owner content. Dedicated rules files (`~/.claude/rules/tokensave.md`, `~/.augment/rules/tokensave.md`, `~/.config/opencode/tokensave.md`) are overwritten only when the bytes actually change. `tokensave doctor` reports, per harness, whether the rules file or block is up to date with the expected version hash, drifted, missing, or still using the legacy heading marker.
- **The worldwide-counter upload now happens at most once a day, from one shared gate.** Four code paths uploaded on four different rules: the CLI attempted a flush on every `init`, `sync` and `status`, and after a 30-second staleness check on everything else; a running MCP server flushed every 30 seconds of tool activity; and its shutdown path uploaded unconditionally. On an active machine that is a synchronous HTTP request per command, for a number that is a single global sum — an intermediate upload moves it for a few hours and is then indistinguishable from having waited. `cloud::upload_is_due` is now the one place the cadence is decided, and every path asks it: enabled, something pending, a day since the last success, and not immediately after a failed attempt. `try_flush`'s `force` parameter is gone rather than made daily, because a command that asks to upload can no longer opt out of the interval. Nothing is lost by declining: saved tokens accumulate locally either way, and the server re-derives its delta from the running total, so the periodic path deliberately does not persist an accumulated figure it did not upload — writing it while the in-process watermark stays put would count the same tokens twice. Two shorter intervals survive, doing narrower jobs: a 60-second back-off after a *failed* attempt, which is what stops a broken network from meaning a request per command (a failure leaves the daily gate open by design), and a 30-second in-process re-entry guard on the server, which only keeps a busy session from re-reading the user config on every tool call. `last_upload_at` was already tracked and is now what actually governs the schedule.
- **The two `sync` consumers of `get_all_edges()` now load only the two edge kinds they use (#418).** `propagate_variant_edges` reads `Annotates`, to find the `#[cfg]`-gated nodes, and `Calls`, to index what is called and which pairs already exist; `type_of`, `returns`, `uses`, `implements`, `extends` and `receives` were carried into both incremental-sync paths and never looked at. On this repository that is 23,167 rows instead of 25,636, and proportionally more on a corpus richer in type and return edges. The `calls` half is irreducible for this algorithm, since any call into a variant member matters. The contract is documented on the function and pinned by a test that adds an edge of every other kind, each shaped so that reading it as gating *would* change the result — the first version of that test used noise the function could not have acted on either way, and passed against a deliberately broken function. With the six sites in the issue, three handlers landed in #430 and these two here; the remaining `handle_gini` keeps its whole-graph load, which #426 confirmed it needs.
- **`tokensave_hotspots` no longer counts a recursive call as connectivity (#431).** A self-edge counted toward *both* in- and out-degree, so a recursive function scored +2 toward the exact quantity the tool ranks by, for a property of its own implementation rather than of how connected it is to anything else. `tokensave_gini`'s fan arms have always excluded self-edges on that reasoning — a recursive call is not a dependant of itself — and the two tools disagreed with nothing in either description saying so. They agree now, in `gini`'s favour. Measured before changing it: this repository holds 405 self-edges out of 25,636, all of them `calls`, and excluding them left the top twelve in identical order with one symbol's degree moving, `run` from 206 to 198. So the ranking is stable in practice and the fix is not a reshuffle. Split out of #418, where preserving the old behaviour was right for a performance change but left the inconsistency standing.
- **Documentation caught up with the resolver as it actually behaves.** `docs/INDEX-DESIGN.md` described Phase 3 as "a scoring heuristic picks the best" candidate, which stopped being true in `e8a8a8a`/`ab9ff29`: every candidate tied for the best score is returned, a lone winner resolves and a tie produces no edge and an `ambiguous_calls` record. The scoring table there was also missing three of its seven signals (directory proximity, language match, import match), the `ambiguous_calls` table was undocumented, and the stated schema version was **5** against an actual **17**. `tokensave_ambiguous_calls` is now listed in the tool tables in `README.md` and `docs/USER-GUIDE.md`, where `dead_code`'s description also notes that ambiguity candidates are excluded from it.
- **Three of `get_all_edges()`'s six consumers no longer materialise the whole edge table (#418).** `handle_hotspots` counted in- and out-degree for all 25,580 edges here, cloning both endpoints of every row, in order to emit at most `limit` rows — 10 by default, 100 at the cap. It now asks SQL for the top `limit` by combined degree. `test_reached_node_ids` and `handle_test_risk` both filtered `EdgeKind::Calls` after loading — `test_risk` on each of three separate passes — and now select that kind in SQL, which drops 6,298 of the 25,580 rows, a quarter of the load, with no change in behaviour. Peak RSS on this repository, three runs each, debug build so not comparable with the release figures in the issue: `hotspots` 40.2 → 37.2 MiB, `complexity` 68.8 → 67.8, `test_risk` 74.8 → 74.3. The aggregate is checked against the in-memory tally it replaces as an oracle, including a self-edge counting toward both degrees, so the ranking is preserved rather than quietly re-derived. **One deliberate behavioural change:** ties are now broken by node id. The tally it replaced sorted a `HashMap`'s iteration order, so `tokensave_hotspots` returned equal-degree nodes in an order that varied between runs on an unchanged index — three runs here produced three different orderings, where the new query produces one. The result set and the degree sequence are identical either way; only the order within a tie group moves, and it moves from arbitrary to fixed. The two `sync` consumers are left alone as #409's territory, and `handle_gini` keeps its whole-graph load, which #426 confirmed it needs: both its file and symbol arms count edges whose far endpoint may sit anywhere in the graph. Reported by @vianbas.
- **Installed agent rules are refreshed from one canonical body per harness (#441).** `tokensave install` and `tokensave reinstall` now rewrite the tokensave rules block in shared global files (`CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `copilot-instructions.md`, `~/.agents/AGENTS.md`) when the canonical text changes, leaving owner content outside the markers untouched. Dedicated rules files (`~/.claude/rules/tokensave.md`, `~/.augment/rules/tokensave.md`, `~/.config/opencode/tokensave.md`) are overwritten only when the body actually changes. The canonical text replaces the hardcoded `.tokensave/tokensave.db` fallback with the active database path from `.tokensave/branch-meta.json`, adds branch freshness and cross-project selector guidance, scopes MCP use to code tasks, and includes a read-only guard. `tokensave doctor` reports per-harness rules version drift, missing blocks, and legacy heading-guarded blocks. Pi now has a documented rules surface at `~/.agents/AGENTS.md`.

### Fixed
- **A `Bash` grep narrowed by an include glob is no longer judged on its search root (#452, #449).** `evaluate_grep_tool_input` has honoured the native `Grep` tool's `glob` field since #279, on the principle that a glob is more specific evidence about what is being searched than the directory the walk starts from. The `Bash` path threw that information away: `extract_grep_invocation` discarded every `-`-prefixed token, so `grep -rn foo --include='*.md' .` was classified from `.`, denied as a code search, and told the caller it "targets a code file" when it targets no code at all. Hit twice in one session while working on the neighbouring half of #452, which is how it was found. `--include` in both spellings, and `rg`'s `-g`/`--glob`/`--iglob` and `-t`/`--type`, now reach the target classifier. The `rg` form was doubly broken: with `-g` unrecognised its value became the *pattern* and the real pattern became a target, so the verdict was about the wrong string entirely. `--exclude` has its value consumed rather than read as a glob, since it says what is *not* being searched. One non-code member of a mixed set passes the whole search through, on the same principle as #448 — the graph cannot answer for it, so blocking costs a round-trip through the opt-out for a query nothing else can serve. Containment is still decided before the glob is consulted, so a code glob cannot pull an out-of-tree path into scope. `Read` deliberately gets no handler and was never in the matcher tokensave installs, so routing and implementation already agreed. Reported by @xaerogonzo.
- **A hand-widened `PreToolUse` matcher survives `install` and `doctor --fix` (#452).** The installed matcher was compared for exact equality with the expected one, so a user who added a tool to `~/.claude/settings.json` silently lost the edit on the next run. A matcher that is a *superset* of what tokensave routes covers everything the handler needs and is no longer treated as drift; a narrower one is still repaired, and the repair now merges rather than replaces, so the `Glob` widening from #389 still reaches existing installs without discarding what the user put there. Reported by @xaerogonzo.
- **The `PreToolUse` grep guardrail now sees a search batched behind another command (#451).** `extract_grep_invocation` required the command to *begin* with `grep`, `rg` or `ag` once its prefix stripper had run, so anything in front of the search hid it completely: `echo "=== A ===" && grep -n SymbolOne src/one.rs`, `printf ready; rg MySymbol src/`, `ls && grep -n MySymbol src/lib.rs` all passed through untouched. That is not a crafted bypass, it is how anyone greps several symbols in one call — a label before each search is the natural way to read the combined output — so the guardrail was silently absent from a common real shape while working on the single-command spelling beside it. The Bash branch now falls back to splitting on top-level `&&`, `||` and `;` and classifying each segment on its own. The whole command is still tried first, unchanged, because that is the only path that models a leading `cd` and resolves the grep target against it; a split would read `cd src` as an ordinary command and lose that. **A denial returns the entire command to the caller unrun**, so the search is redirected only when every *other* segment is inert — `echo`, `pwd`, `ls`, `cat`, `printf`, and nothing else. `true` and `:` are inert *only ahead of* the search, where they carry no work of their own, so `true; grep -n Sym src/lib.rs` is still a batched search; after it they are consuming the search's exit status, and `grep -n Sym src/lib.rs || true` is how a failing search is stopped from failing a script, which #475/#476 pin as pass-through. Anything unrecognized counts as doing real work, which makes the failure direction allow: `git checkout -b x && rg Sym src/` still passes through, because blocking it would throw away the checkout to save a grep. The same reasoning leaves subshells, command substitution, newline-separated command lists, pipes, redirects and background jobs out of scope — the hook does not model what they run, so it does not act on them — and a segment that is a search but whose pattern is not symbol-shaped is still the pattern classifier's call, not the splitter's. Reported by @dangeReis.
- **A running MCP server now actually exits on `SIGTERM`, instead of shutting down gracefully and then sitting there alive (#450, and the reapability half of #436).** The reported symptom was that a plain `kill` did nothing — the process was still alive and still burning CPU seconds later, and `SIGKILL` was required, which loses the graceful shutdown's WAL checkpoint. Measured here with stdin held open the way a live supervisor holds it: the server ran its full shutdown, printed its `[tokensave] shutdown: … uptime 5s` summary, and was **still running 30 seconds later**. Two separate defects, both ours rather than the host's. First, `tokio::io::stdin()` performs its reads on a blocking thread, and a blocking task cannot be cancelled; the outstanding read is therefore still parked when the run loop leaves, and dropping the runtime waits for it. Under a supervisor that never closes our stdin — exactly the Codex case in #436 — that wait never ends, so the only thing that had ever *really* stopped these servers was stdin closing, and signals only appeared to work when EOF happened to follow. The serve path now flushes stdout and exits explicitly once shutdown returns, which is safe because shutdown is idempotent and has already persisted counters and checkpointed the WAL. Second, the `SIGTERM` stream was constructed *inside* the run loop, so it existed only while `select!` awaited the next line and was dropped before `handle_request` ran; tokio's registration is process-global and is not undone on drop, so after the first iteration the default disposition — terminate — was permanently replaced while, for most of a busy server's life, nothing was listening. A signal delivered during request handling was neither fatal nor observable: the next iteration built a fresh stream, which cannot see an event delivered before it existed. The stream is now registered once for the life of the loop, so it coalesces and retains a signal arriving mid-request and the server leaves at the top of the next iteration. Interrupting a long in-flight sync rather than waiting for it is a separate decision and stays open on #450. Pinned by `tests/serve_sigterm_test.rs`, which drives a real `serve` process through one request — the state in which the signal used to be lost — then `SIGTERM`s it with stdin still open; it hangs for the full 20-second budget on the previous code and exits immediately now. Reported by @not-antoni and @jasonb194.
- **`tokensave cost --export json` now publishes every priced token category, so the exported tokens can account for the exported cost (#472).** The payload carried `total_input_tokens` and `total_output_tokens` but no cache field at all, while `total_cost_usd` was computed from the full usage record — so dividing cost by tokens implied roughly $270–$360 per million, several times any published Anthropic rate, and the export disagreed with the tool's own `--by-agent` view about token volume by around 600× while agreeing with it on cost to within rounding. Two further symptoms pointed at the same cause: `sum(by_model[].tokens)` equalled `total_input + total_output` exactly, so there was nowhere in the payload for cache tokens to be hiding; and the export showed about 455 output tokens per input token, a ratio only possible if most input is uncounted, since agent workloads are strongly input-heavy — a large context is resent every turn and the completion is comparatively small. That resent context is a cache read: priced, dominant, and absent from the export. `token_breakdown_since` now returns cache creation alongside cache reads, the summary carries both, and the JSON gains `total_cache_read_tokens`, `total_cache_creation_tokens` and a `total_tokens` that sums all four. `cost_by_model_since` counts the same four categories, so the payload's own cross-check — summing `by_model[].tokens` — reconciles with the totals instead of silently agreeing with a wrong number. Reported by @xaerogonzo.
- **`tokensave cost <RANGE> --export json` now reports a `tokens_saved` that belongs to the range it was asked about, and agrees with `tokensave gain` (#473).** The field was byte-identical for every range — the savings figure for `today` equalled the one for `all` — because the caller sourced it from `global_tokens_saved()`, a lifetime, all-projects counter read from the `projects` table, and dropped it into a payload where everything around it was range-scoped. `efficiency_ratio` is derived from it and inherited the fault in a way that looked like a measurement rather than an artefact: a fixed numerator divided by a growing denominator falls monotonically as the range widens, so the ratio appeared to say something about longer horizons while describing nothing that changed. The same counter also disagreed with `gain`, which reads the `savings_ledger` and scopes correctly, by roughly 2.15× on the reporter's machine — two different "tokens saved" numbers for what a reader takes to be one quantity at one scope. `cost_summary` now derives the figure itself from the `since` the rest of the summary already uses, reading the same ledger `gain` reads, rather than accepting it as an argument: the parameter is gone, so a caller can no longer pass a value scoped differently from the summary it lands in, and the two commands cannot drift apart by source. The human table's `Savings X tokens (N% efficiency)` line is fixed by the same change. This matters beyond the JSON because `cost` is where a consumer naturally looks for a savings figure, and nothing in the payload distinguished the two scopes — a UI reading `tokens_saved` from a `cost 7d` response reported a lifetime total under a "past 7 days" heading with no way to know it had. Reported by @xaerogonzo.
- **The grep guardrail no longer denies a command because a search happens to come first (#475).** `extract_grep_invocation` only required the line to *start* with `grep`/`rg`/`ag` once the known wrappers were peeled, and then read everything after the tool name as that grep's own arguments — so a trailing `&& git status` or `&& ./deploy.sh` was invisible to it. `grep -n MySymbol src/lib.rs && ./deploy.sh` was denied as a bare code search, and because a denial returns the *whole* command unrun, the deploy was discarded along with the search. That is the expensive direction of the same blind spot #451 reports in its silent direction, and it inverts what the guardrail is for: it exists to suggest a cheaper way to answer a question, so it must never destroy work it cannot see. A search chained to anything else — `&&`, `||`, `;`, `|`, or a backgrounding `&`, tracked outside quotes so an operator inside a pattern or a quoted argument still reads as one search — now passes through untouched, and the same rule governs the `find`/`fd` discovery path, which had the identical shape. The unchained search is still redirected, and a leading `cd … &&` is still peeled as prefix noise rather than read as a chain. Reported by @dangeReis.
- **The `PreToolUse` grep guardrail no longer blocks paths the project's own config excludes from indexing (#448).** #435 fixed the out-of-tree half of this: a grep whose target resolved outside the project root is not tokensave's concern. The in-tree half stayed broken, because "inside the tree" is not "inside the index" — a project's `.tokensave/config.json` `exclude` globs keep `node_modules`, `vendor`, `build`, `target` and the rest out of the graph entirely, and the guardrail only ever asked about containment in the root. So `grep -n someExportedName node_modules/some-package/lib/index.d.ts` was blocked and redirected to `tokensave_search`, which returns nothing for it by construction: the caller was sent from a command that works to a tool that cannot answer, with `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK=1` as the only way through. Every grep against vendored or generated code inside the tree paid that round-trip. The hook now resolves the target against the same `exclude` globs the indexer uses, read from the project's own config rather than a second list kept beside it, so the two cannot drift; both glob spellings are checked (`vendor/**` and `**/vendor`), since a grep target is as likely to name a directory as a file, and relative and absolute spellings are canonicalized alike. A config that cannot be read leaves the previous rules in charge, the same fail-open policy the containment check already applies to a path it cannot resolve — and with nothing excluded, behavior is unchanged. Reported by @not-antoni.
- **Every registered MCP tool now has an explicit tracked-branch drift classification.** A registry invariant partitions tools into selector-capable, selector-less refused, and explicitly exempt sets, so adding a tool cannot silently bypass drift handling. Runtime refusal behavior and selector schemas are unchanged.
- **Status now identifies the canonical project root selected by the CLI or MCP server (#437).** `tokensave_status` includes the absolute root as the string field `project_root`, and both normal and short `tokensave status` headers show the same `TokenSave::project_root()` value while preserving the existing branch row.
- **`tokensave_field_sites` reported a capped page as a total, so a blast radius could be silently understated (#457).** `write_count` and `read_count` were the length of the returned array *after* the `limit` cap, so they tracked the limit exactly — the reporter's back-to-back calls on the same field returned `write_count: 20` at `limit: 20` and `21` at `limit: 21` — and nothing in the response distinguished "there are 21" from "here are the first 21 of more". This is the tool whose entire purpose is "if I change this field, how many places are affected?", and the default `limit` of 200 made the number look authoritative for anything under it. The scan now counts every matching site and stops only *listing* once the cap is reached: `write_count`/`read_count` are the true totals, the new `write_returned`/`read_returned` are how many are listed, and `truncated` plus a `truncation_note` say when those differ. Also from that report, one source line holding two references to the field yields two entries with identical `file`, `line` and `snippet` — defensible as raw data, but it left the count neither a site count nor a line count, which is how a downstream consumer produced 8 and 4 for the same query, both labelled complete. Sites and distinct lines are now separate named quantities (`write_count` vs `write_lines`), so no caller has to invent its own deduplication. The cost is that a capped query now scans the remaining files instead of breaking early; that is the price of the total being real, and the uncapped default was already scanning them. Reported by @not-antoni.
- **`tokensave_field_sites` accepted a `Type::field` qualifier, ignored it, and returned the broad answer under a narrow heading (#458).** The tool's own description promised that "`Struct::field` form narrows to a specific declaration", and the handler parsed the qualifier and then assigned `qualifier_applied = false` unconditionally — the sites returned were always the bare field name's, across every struct. The `false` flag was honest and machine-readable, but it is easy to miss and the failure runs in the dangerous direction: the caller explicitly asked to narrow, so the result *looks* narrowed because that is what was requested. One downstream tool ended up refusing the query outright on seeing the flag, which made a documented feature unusable. Narrowing a text site to one struct needs the receiver's resolved type, which this source-text pass deliberately does not compute, so what changes here is the honesty rather than the scan: the tool description and the `field` parameter now state that the qualified form is accepted but **not** applied, and a qualified call comes back with a `qualifier_note` saying in words that the sites cover every same-named field and why. Whether to implement real narrowing, or to reject the qualified form outright, is left open on the issue. Reported by @not-antoni.
- **A literal search now says when it could not look inside every tracked file, instead of returning a partial answer as a complete one (#442).** `tokensave_search` with `literal: true` reads bytes rather than symbols, so it needs no parser — but it iterates the `files` table, so it reaches a file only when the index holds a row for it: one an extractor handles, or one whose extension is listed in `artifact_extensions` (#323). A tracked `.html` template or `.css` stylesheet is usually neither, and those are exactly where a flag's user-facing label, a route, or a class name lives, so `count: 8` was returned for a project that held more, with nothing to indicate a whole class of files had never been scanned. The reported consequence is the sharp one: an agent instructed to prefer tokensave over `grep` gets a confidently incomplete answer and no signal to fall back. The response now carries an `unscanned` block giving the number of tracked files the index holds no row for, a per-extension breakdown of them (capped at eight rows, the rest rolled into `more_extensions`), and the remedy — which is a real one rather than a workaround, since adding the extension to `artifact_extensions` and running `sync -f` makes those files literal-searchable, reported with `enclosing: null` where there is no symbol context. That path already worked and was simply undocumented, so `README.md` and `docs/USER-GUIDE.md` now say so, the latter documenting literal mode for the first time; the `sync` skipped-extension hint names the setting too, where it previously said only that no extractor was registered and left the user with nowhere to go. Two deliberate scoping choices. The tally is **not** filtered through `NON_SOURCE_EXTS`: that list exists to keep asset noise out of the skipped-*language* diagnostic and contains `txt`, `xml`, `ini`, `conf` and `csv`, which are precisely the text formats a literal search might be hunting in, so filtering by it would have recreated the same silent gap one level down — every unindexed extension is reported, including `.png`, and the caller decides which are worth adding. And a file the caller excluded is never reported: config `exclude` globs, project query-ignore, and the call's own `path_include`/`path_exclude` and scope prefix all apply to the report as well as to the scan, so it names only files that were asked about and could not be answered for. The enumeration is one `git ls-files` (4 ms over this repository's 564 tracked files, against a scan that reads every indexed file's bytes), shared with `detect_skipped_hidden_dirs` rather than duplicating its subprocess. `None` when git cannot answer, so "cannot tell" never renders as "nothing missing". Reported by @not-antoni.
- **Python: a function called from module scope, or a method passed by reference through `self`, was reported by `tokensave_dead_code`.** Two extractor misses. First, call sites were extracted only from function bodies, so `_KEYMAP = _build_keymap()`, a bare `_setup()`, or a call under `if __name__ == "__main__":`, `try`, `match`, `assert`, or `raise` gave the callee no incoming `calls` edge. Every module- and class-scope statement now runs the same call-site extraction, attributed to the File node or the enclosing class. Second, the value-position scan that turns `invoke(callback=_cb)` into a `uses` edge stopped at every `attribute` node, so `Thread(target=self._flush_loop)` or `schedule(self._tick, 1.0)` produced nothing for the method. A `self.name`/`cls.name` read in a value position now emits a `uses` ref when `name` is a method the enclosing class defines and never binds as an attribute — `self.status = 1` in one of its own methods, or `status = None` in the class body, makes a later `self.status` a field read, so a `status` method is still reported dead; a nested class's `self` is a different receiver and does not count, and an assignment to a `@property`/`.setter` descriptor invokes it rather than shadowing it. Also at definition time: a call in a parameter default (`def f(x=_build())`), in a class header (`class C(_make_base()):`), or in a decorator's arguments (`@_register(_build())`) now gives the callee a `calls` edge, and a bare-name decorator (`@_register`) gives `_register` one; a dotted decorator callee (`@app.route`) is left alone, since its trailing segment would bind to any same-named symbol. The ref carries the class's full qualified name (`a.py::Daemon::_flush_loop`), so it resolves to that class's method and not to a same-named class in another file. `dead_code` already counts `uses` as liveness, so no query change was needed. Pinned by `tests/python_module_scope_refs_test.rs`, which runs the real tool over a tempdir project; every case fails on the previous extractor, and the cross-file case puts the unreferenced class in the file that indexes first so a first-suffix-match resolver would bind to the wrong one.
- **A running MCP server now refuses local graph queries after a tracked-branch checkout, instead of answering from the branch it opened (#446).** A long-lived server binds to one branch's database at startup, and `git checkout` underneath it does not change which database it holds. The write side of that drift has refused since #400/#403 — automatic sync will not write branch B's files into branch A's index — but the read side answered anyway, with a warning prefix, so `search`, `context`, `callers`, `impact` and every analysis tool returned plausible results describing the wrong branch. Worse, deleting the served branch left its database answering with symbols that no longer existed anywhere. Local graph tools now fail closed with an `InvalidRequest` naming the serving branch, the working-tree branch, and the recovery action; `tokensave_status` stays callable so the situation can be diagnosed from inside the drifted session. **This changes a default**: drift was advisory unless `strict_tree` was set, and is now refused for everyone. The reasoning is that the two halves of one condition disagreed — a refusal to write and a warning-prefixed answer to read — and a warning is not a control an agent instructed to prefer tokensave over reading files can act on. `strict_tree` keeps its remaining job, the separate different-*worktree* mismatch, which is still advisory by default. Single-database projects are unaffected, since one database is shared by design, and an explicit `graph_root`/`graph_branch` selection is a read-only snapshot that never reaches the gate. Reported and fixed by @rNoz.
- **The `PreToolUse` grep guardrail no longer blocks searches that target paths outside the indexed project (#435).** The guardrail used to ask only "does the target look like code?" and redirect any symbol-shaped grep whose file extension or directory looked indexed, even when the resolved path was in `/tmp`, a sibling repo, or a vendored reference tree. It now asks "is the target inside the project *and* does it look like code?" by canonicalizing concrete paths against the project root. A leading `cd <dir> && grep ...` is re-rooted at the cd'd directory, so `cd /other/repo && grep -rn Symbol .` passes through, and `find`/`fd` commands get the same re-rooting. Backslash-escaped cd paths are resolved to the real directory, and a symlink to a code directory is classified by its canonical target. The fail-open policy for paths that cannot be resolved is shared by a single `Containment` helper, so unresolvable targets fall through to the existing extension/directory rules rather than being treated as outside. Only a single leading `cd` is modeled; if a command contains multiple leading `cd` prefixes, only the first one is used and the rest are treated as noise. Reported by @jasonb194.
- **`graph_root`'s union type is now spelled `anyOf`, because a `type` array made every Gemini-backed request fail wholesale (#440).** The parameter accepts either a single root or an array of them (#376), and that was declared as `"type": ["string", "array"]` with an `items` schema. JSON Schema permits the union; Gemini's `function_declarations` proto does not — when `items` is present it validates `type` as exactly `Type.ARRAY`, and a union cannot be expressed as a single `Type` enum. Gemini validates the whole `GenerateContentRequest` up front, so the 52 tokensave tools carrying the selector rejected all 354 tool declarations in the reporter's session with an HTTP 400 naming `properties[graph_root].items`: not a degraded tokensave, but no tool calls at all, for any server in the session. `{"anyOf": [{"type": "string"}, {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}}]}` states the same constraint and passes, which @sruckh verified A/B against the same model and endpoint with one tool and everything else held identical. Nothing about what callers may pass changes, and the guard test is now two assertions rather than one — the `anyOf` shape exactly, plus the absence of any `type` key on the property, so a future edit cannot regress to the union that broke this. Reported by @sruckh, who diagnosed it to the proto predicate and supplied the fix.
- **A C++ header was parsed with the C grammar, and a macro-decorated declaration was not parsed at all, so a `.h` yielded no class, method or field node.** `.h` is the one extension naming no single language, and it was routed by extension alone; on top of that, `class MYLIB_API AFoo` reparses as a function definition, so even the C++ grammar would have seen no class. UE, Chromium, Qt and LLVM headers all spell both shapes, so the two faults compounded on exactly the trees where the headers carry the API. A `.h` is now routed by its source dialect — C++ markers, Objective-C markers, or plain C — and declaration macros are blanked space-for-byte before parsing, which keeps every offset, line and column addressing the real file, so stored coordinates, fingerprints and blame all still land where they did. A body or a brace initializer is deliberately left alone: a `MACRO(...)` there is a call, and blanking it would delete call edges. Two shapes are handled explicitly because each one otherwise loses code with no error: a caps-named *type* is not a macro, so `MYLIB_API const int x` is blanked while `HANDLE const h` is not — the test is whether a type is still named past the qualifier, and getting this wrong deletes the declaration outright; and raw string literals and digit separators are lexed, since `R"(a")"` or `1'000` would otherwise desync the scan and leave every macro after them in the file unblanked. Which braces hold declarations rather than code is decided by the keyword that opened them - `class`, `struct`, `union`, `enum`, `namespace`, `extern` - and not by the punctuation before the `{`, because a body opened past a trailing specifier (`void TearDown() override {`, `int Size() const {`, `auto Get() -> int {`) is code and the calls in it must survive; reading it as a declaration scope blanked the `EXPECT_EQ(1, n1_)` inside it. For the same reason a member initializer (`: MD(md)`) and a qualified name are not access labels, and a macro that IS the declaration keeps everything it owns - a body (`TEST(S, C) {}`), a second parameter list (COM's `STDMETHOD(Read)(args) override {}`) or a lambda argument (`INIT_FUNCTION(Name, [] { Reset(); })`). **A template layer's implementation headers are now indexed at all.** `supported_extensions()` is built from the extractors, and `CppExtractor` claimed neither `.inl`, `.ipp` nor `.tcc`, so those files were never walked - while `hooks.rs` already listed `.ipp` and `.tcc` among the extensions tokensave indexes, and `is_header_path` already treated all three as headers. They are C++ by construction, so they route by extension with no dialect sniff, and they map to `cpp` in `lang_from_path` and `ts_lang_key_from_path` too: #346 measured an unmapped extension as exempt from both cross-language guards, scoring it above the correct same-language candidate. Measured on two template-heavy trees at their current heads: asio gains 1,145 nodes over 75 files with edges 10,805 -> 13,002, and glm 2,989 nodes over 140 files with edges 1,560 -> 3,242. Routing headers to the C++ extractor also exposed five class-body members it had been dropping, each now extracted: preprocessor-guarded members (both arms, as file scope already did), a `friend` definition, a function-pointer member (previously an anonymous method), a nested type, and a `using` alias. Resolution scores C and C++ as one language family — a `.cpp` calling into its own `.h` scored 0.5, under the 0.6 floor, and lost the edge — and prefers the definition over the header declaration once both exist. Measured over a 230-file UE-style C++ codebase, 112 of them headers: header symbols 886 → 2604 (0 → 76 classes, 0 → 760 methods, 296 → 1105 fields, 0 → 33 enums), nodes 3663 → 5381, edges 1903 → 2032, and signatures carrying a newline, tab or run of spaces 356 → 0. Every name the old index carried and the new one does not is a type name the C grammar had recorded as a symbol — `UTextBlock`, `int32`, `GENERATED_BODY` — so nothing real is traded for the gain. **A `.h` carrying no C++ and no Objective-C marker still routes to C**, which is right for a C header and is the one case where the dialect is genuinely unknowable from the text. Cross-checked over five public C++ trees at their current heads (fmt, nlohmann/json, googletest, asio, wil), 2,059 C-family files: blanking preserves every file's length, line count and character boundaries, is idempotent, and every span it rewrites reads back as a caps-named macro with at most its own argument list - no span holds a `;` or a `{`. Indexing all five before and after these three rules gains 85 nodes and 67 edges and loses none; the only kind that moves down is a COM `STDMETHOD` member, now read as a method rather than a free function (442 -> 480 methods against 1067 -> 1060 functions in one tree).
- **A background config refresh no longer announces itself as a bare `✔ Wrote <path>` from under a read-only command (#419).** The trigger is now identified, and it is the one @xaerogonzo guessed. `Gitignore` is not in `should_skip_agent_install_maintenance`, so a documented query runs the startup maintenance block, where `resync_installed_agents` re-installs every tracked agent when the running version is newer than `last_installed_version` — the fallback for an external upgrade, exactly the in-place 7.9.0 → 7.10.0 he described. That resync sets `set_quiet_install(true)` to keep per-agent banners off stderr, but `write_json_file` and `write_toml_file` printed `✔ Wrote {path}` through a raw `eprintln!` rather than the quiet-gated `agent_note!`, so the one line that named a file escaped while all its context was suppressed. It fired exactly once because the resync advances `last_installed_version`, which is why it never reproduced. The two write lines now honour quiet mode like every other install message, and the resync prints one line saying what it did and why: `✔ Refreshed agent config for tokensave 7.10.0 (was 7.9.0) — 1 agent`. A user-run `tokensave install` is unaffected, since quiet mode is off there and the per-file lines still print. Also worth recording for anyone who goes looking: `last_installed_version` and `previous_version` live in `~/.tokensave/state.toml`, not `config.toml`, which is why the version stamp he searched for was not where he looked.
- **`gini --metric lines --scope file` reported a file's length as the sum of every node's span inside it, so `src/types.rs` came back as 2,104 lines against an actual 849 (#422).** The graph holds a node per struct, field, enum variant, impl block and method, and they nest, so one physical line was counted once for every node enclosing it — and the `file` node's own full span was one of the terms, so each file's length was added on top of the symbols within it. The arm now reports the `file` node's own span, which matches `wc -l`, with `max(end_line)` as a fallback for a file that has no `file` node (no language indexed today produces one). This was not a harmless constant: across the 452 files here the inflation ran from 1.00x to 3.45x, median 1.96x, so it did not cancel out of a scale-invariant coefficient, and it reordered the ranking the metric exists to produce. The top two files swapped places and only 6 of the 10 files were common to both lists; `src/mcp/tools/definitions.rs` and `src/mcp/tools/handlers/analysis.rs` are genuinely among the ten longest and were missing from the reported one. The three longest files now report 6,528, 4,988 and 3,504 lines, each equal to `wc -l`. Reported by @vianbas, who measured the distortion and deliberately left the choice of definition open; the file's own length is the reading that matches the tool's stated purpose of finding god files.
- **`tokensave_health`'s `equality` dimension counted every file's length twice over (#422).** `compute_health_snapshot` summed each node's line span into per-file complexity, the `file` node included, so a file scored its own length on top of the spans of its symbols — and a file with no extracted symbols at all scored purely its length, ranking a comment-only shell script alongside dense code. File nodes carry no branches, loops or nesting, so they contributed nothing but the duplicated span and are now skipped, while every file keeps an entry so a symbol-free one still ranks, at 0. On this repository `equality` moves from 0.4006 to 0.3621, reproduced to four places by independent arithmetic over the same index. A regression test pins the invariant that a symbol-free file's length cannot move the score: before this change, growing a comment-only file from 20 lines to 400 moved `equality` from 0.9737 to 0.5431.
- **File-scope `fan_in` and `fan_out` dropped every edge crossing a `--path` boundary (#423, #422).** The arms mapped edge endpoints to files using only the path-filtered nodes, so an edge whose other end lived outside the prefix resolved to nothing and was discarded — a scoped file's fan-in counted only its intra-prefix callers, and the whole-graph `get_all_edges()` load above it was wasted. The comment there states the intent plainly: fan-in of a scoped node counts edges from outside it. The endpoint map now spans the graph while only files inside the prefix are ranked, matching the symbol-scope arms added for #417. Under `--path src/mcp`, `src/mcp/tools/handlers/mod.rs` now reports 490 incoming edges against 271 before, and 490 is what the same count taken directly in SQL over the whole graph gives.
- **A config write that would not change a byte no longer rewrites the file, backs it up, or announces itself (#419).** `tokensave gitignore` with no ACTION is documented as a query — "omit to show current setting" — and it printed `✔ Wrote ~/.claude/settings.json`, advanced that file's mtime and left a fresh `settings.json.bak` beside it, with content byte-identical to what was already there. Nothing was lost, but `settings.json` holds the agent's permissions, hooks and MCP wiring: rewriting it races anything else with the file open, and an interruption mid-write turns a no-op into a truncated settings file. A user running a query has quiesced nothing, because they had no reason to expect a write. `write_json_file` and `backup_and_write_json` now compare the rendered bytes against what is on disk and return early when they match — no backup, no rename, no message — and `safe_write_json_file` carries the same guard for the call sites that back up themselves and call straight through. Comparison is on rendered bytes rather than parsed JSON deliberately: writing normalises key order, indentation and the trailing newline, so a formatting-only rewrite is still a real change to a file the user may track in git, and only an exact match is treated as a no-op. This covers every agent integration, since all of them write through these three functions. The condition that made a query reach a settings write at all was not reproducible from the outside and is still open on #419; what is fixed is that reaching it with unchanged content now costs nothing. Reported by @xaerogonzo, who also proposed this fix.
- **`tokensave_gini` answered three of its ten metric and scope pairs with complexity, labelled as the metric asked for (#417).** At `scope: "symbol"` only `members` had a match arm of its own, so `lines`, `fan_in` and `fan_out` fell through to the catch-all `(_, "symbol")` arm, which computes per-symbol complexity — the response carried the requested metric name and complexity's numbers, with nothing to say the metric had not been measured. Measured on this repository at `0651c0a`: all four of `complexity`, `lines`, `fan_in` and `fan_out` returned a coefficient of 0.5768 over the same 7,673 symbols; they now return 0.5768, 0.4994, 0.6718 and 0.5681. The two new arms follow the file-scope arms directly above them — `lines` is the node's line span, fan-in and fan-out count every edge kind, and an edge whose endpoints are the same symbol is not counted, so a recursive call is not a dependant of itself. The top five symbols of each metric agree exactly with the same counts taken directly in SQL against the index. `complexity` and `members` are unchanged, and the catch-all stays for a metric outside the declared enum. These arms differ from the file-scope arms under a `path` filter, and the file-scope arms are the ones that are wrong: they build their node-to-file map from the already path-filtered nodes, so an edge with an endpoint outside the prefix is dropped, which also makes the whole-graph edge load directly above them pointless when a prefix is set. The symbol arms follow the stated contract instead — a scoped symbol's fan-in counts its callers wherever they live — so under `--path src/mcp`, `handle_tool_call` has 221 incoming edges rather than the 3 the file arms would count. Fixing the file arms is left to a follow-up, alongside #422.
- **A method declared without a body in an `abstract class` never became a node, so calls into it lost their edge (#424).** `abstract render(): string;` parses as `abstract_method_signature`, and `visit_class_body` matched only `decorator`, `method_definition` and `public_field_definition`, so the declaration fell through the catch-all arm and nothing at all was recorded for it. The identical bodiless shape inside an `interface` is extracted, by `visit_interface_body`, which is why the inconsistency did not show from the outside. `AbstractMethod` was already a node kind, already accepted by the resolver's `Calls` gate, and already emitted by the Java and Kotlin extractors for the same construct; the TypeScript extractor was the one that never produced it. The consequence appears at the call site as soon as the base has more than one subclass: with no declaration to resolve to, the resolver sees only the implementations, cannot separate them, and refuses the tie, so the call produces no edge and `tokensave_callees` on the caller returns nothing. With exactly one subclass the call still lands on that implementation by name, which is why the shape hid behind the #413 fixtures, where every method has a body. Measured across three TypeScript corpora at their current heads, counting `MethodDeclaration` members with no body on classes carrying the `abstract` modifier, tests and `.d.ts` and `declare` contexts excluded: nestjs/nest has 51 across 10 abstract classes, typeorm/typeorm 9 across 6, moeru-ai/airi 2 in 1, and the index held zero `abstract_method` nodes from `.ts` in all three — the only ones present anywhere in those trees come from a Kotlin file. The declaration now becomes an `AbstractMethod` node carrying its accessibility, JSDoc and `Contains` edge, and a call through a base-typed receiver resolves to it rather than to either implementation. One consequence is a change in existing output rather than a pure addition: with a *single* subclass the call already resolved, to that implementation, and now resolves to the declaration instead — the honest answer at a base-typed call site, and the same way an interface-typed receiver already behaves, but `tokensave_callees` on such a caller returns a different symbol than it did in 7.10.0. Nothing links a declaration to its implementations, since the only edge between the classes is `extends` at class level, so a `callees` walk now stops at the declaration; adding an `overrides` edge to make that navigable in both directions is left open. This does not produce `dead_code` false positives for the implementations — verified against a fixture also holding a deliberately dead exported function, where the count stayed 0. Reported and fixed by @vianbas.

## [7.10.0] - 2026-08-19

### Changed
- **The last order-dependent pick in the resolver is gone (#412).** `resolve_from_filtered_named` — the path taken whenever the kind filter shrinks the candidate list — still chose "the first candidate in the reference's own file, else the first overall", with a comment saying so, because scoring wanted a shape it did not have. It did not: `find_best_matches` already accepts the borrowed slice, so it now scores like every other multi-candidate path, and a tie produces no edge and is recorded in `ambiguous_calls` rather than resolved by scan order. Measured by indexing a clean clone of this repository before and after: call edges 19,356 → 19,213 (-143, 0.7%), ambiguity records 10,985 → 11,148 (+163). Small because `e8a8a8a` had already removed the large tie population from the primary paths; what remains is the guarantee — no resolution in the graph now depends on file enumeration order. The guarantee is now pinned by `tests/resolution_order_independence_test.rs`, which indexes the same source twice with the candidate definitions swapped between files and compares the resulting edges. Unlike the earlier fixture in `ts_phantom_call_edges_test.rs`, which passed before the fix as well as after, the kind-filtered case there genuinely reproduces: against the previous resolver it resolves to `Handler::process` one way round and `Worker::process` the other.

- **Score ties no longer resolve to whichever candidate the file scan reached first (#412).** `find_best_match` returned one winner using `if score > best_score`, so when several candidates tied on every scoring dimension the first one reached won — and "first" is file enumeration order, which made the graph a function of the filesystem rather than of the source. It is now `find_best_matches`, returning every candidate tied for the best score, and all three call sites apply one rule: a lone winner resolves, a tie produces no edge and is recorded in `ambiguous_calls` with its candidates.
