The special-case `find_best_match_unambiguous` added for the bare-name fallback is gone — the honest behaviour is now the default rather than one path's opt-in. **The cost is real and language-dependent**, measured by indexing two corpora before and after: on this repository (Rust) call edges fall 22,502 → 19,309, a 14.2% loss, with ambiguity records rising 7,653 → 10,930; on a 1,332-file TypeScript corpus they fall 40,266 → 39,888, 0.9%, with records rising 2,633 → 3,011. Nothing is discarded — the edges reappear as candidate lists almost one for one. Much of the Rust figure is fabrication being withdrawn rather than fact being lost: the largest group is 1,637 `is_empty` calls, and the codebase defines exactly two local `is_empty` methods, so calls to `Vec::is_empty` were being bound to one of them arbitrarily — the phantom-edge class #378 reported, at scale. `Vec::new`, `fs::write`, `serde_json::from_str` and `TempDir::new` behave the same way. **The split has since been measured** by diffing the two indexes edge by edge: of the 3,971 `calls` edges the Rust index loses, **2,845 (71.6%) pointed from one file at a `private` item in a different file** — a call Rust cannot express, so those edges were provably fabricated. The remainder are `pub` targets, and sampling them finds the same class rather than a different one: every one of twelve inspected is a standard-library method call (`as_str` on a `String`, 680 edges) that had been bound to an unrelated private helper — in that case `as_str` in `src/accounting/discover.rs`, reached from test files that do not import it. Only 4 of the 3,971 were same-file. The 14.2% is therefore dominated by withdrawn fabrication, not by lost fact, though the `pub` remainder is characterised by sampling rather than exhaustively.

### Fixed
- **`abstract class` declarations were never extracted, taking the class, its methods, and every edge into them (#413).** An abstract class parses as `abstract_class_declaration`, a different node kind from `class_declaration`, and the extractor dispatched only on the latter — in both the top-level and `export` paths. Nothing wrong was recorded; the symbols simply never reached the graph, so every question about them answered "no" with the same confidence as a real negative. In TypeScript an abstract base is usually where the shared interface lives, so this removed exactly the methods most likely to be called, and left every concrete subclass's `extends` pointing at nothing. On the reporter's fixture the index held 2 of the expected 6 class and method nodes. Reported by @vianbas.
- **A constructor parameter property declared no field type, so calls through it did not resolve (#414).** `constructor(private readonly worker: Beta) {}` declares a field, but the declaration sits in the parameter list rather than the class body, so the field scan added in `3b294bc` never saw it — leaving the standard dependency-injection form, and the one most likely to appear in the code #378 was reported against, still falling through to the bare-name fallback. The accessibility or `readonly` modifier is what distinguishes a property from an ordinary constructor argument, so an unmodified parameter stays local to the constructor rather than becoming a field. Reported by @vianbas.

### Added
- **Ruby `attr_reader`/`attr_writer`/`attr_accessor` now emit method nodes.** A receiverless `attr_*` call in a class or module body is indexed the same way an equivalent `def` would be — same kind, visibility, parent, and `Contains` edge — including inside `class << self` and Concern `included`/`class_methods` blocks. Previously these accessors were invisible to search, callers, and dead-code analysis.

### Added
- **Ruby `alias`/`alias_method` now emit method nodes.** A receiverless `alias`/`alias_method` in a class, module, or `class << self` body is indexed the same way an equivalent `def` would be, with visibility copied from the source method. Previously these methods were invisible to search, `callers`, and dead-code analysis, and calls to them were permanently unresolvable.

### Added
- **`tokensave_ambiguous_calls`: the candidates behind a call the resolver could not pin down (#412).** #378 stopped fabricating an edge when several targets tied, which was right — an edge asserts a specific target and "one of these" is not one — but it discarded what the resolver knew. The candidates are now recorded (schema v17, `ambiguous_calls`) and readable, so the ambiguity becomes data rather than silence. A model has the source in front of it and can usually tell which `dispose` was meant from the receiver's type, which a scoring heuristic cannot see; it can only do that if it is told there was a choice. Each call site is returned with its unresolved reference and every candidate's name, kind, file, line and qualified name, scoped by `path` and capped (default 25, max 200 — a hard cap, not a page, since a large codebase can hold thousands). **`dead_code` now excludes symbols named as an ambiguity candidate**: having refused the edge, reporting the target as uncalled would trade a fabricated edge for a fabricated finding, and a finding reads as more actionable than an edge — the same mistake #346 measured at a 97% false-positive rate, in the other direction. No edges are created, so the refusal's guarantee is intact. Ambiguity is computed only for references that already failed to resolve, so the cost falls on a minority of refs rather than threading state through the parallel resolution pass.

### Fixed
- **A call through a typed field now resolves to that field's class instead of being ambiguous (#412, option C).** `collect_var_types` tracked bare `this`, typed parameters and typed bindings, but never `this.<field>` — so `this.statusBar.dispose()` reached the bare-name fallback with nothing to separate two classes that both define `dispose`, and #378 had to refuse the edge rather than guess. The field's type is in the source either way: an annotation (`private worker: Beta`, the constructor-injection shape) or an initializer (`private statusBar = new StatusBar()`), with the annotation winning when both are present. The reference becomes `StatusBar::dispose` and binds precisely, recovering an edge the refusal had to give up rather than trading recall for precision. Receivers whose type genuinely cannot be known — an untyped parameter, say — still produce no edge when several candidates tie. No measurable effect on this repository, which is Rust and never reaches the TypeScript path; validated by targeted tests on the reported shape rather than by a corpus measurement.

### Added
- **`graph_root` accepts an array, so one query can be answered across several projects (#376).** `tokensave_search` and `tokensave_files` fan out across every root named, run the ordinary selected-graph pipeline per root, and interleave the results round-robin by rank with a 25-entry per-root cap. Rank rather than score, because BM25 scores are per-database and not calibrated between them — sorting two roots' scores together compares numbers that do not share a scale. Every other tool answers about a single graph and now rejects an array with an explanation, since a union of two graphs is not a larger answer but a meaningless one. **Roots that are worktrees of a repository already named are collapsed first**, using the shared `git rev-parse --git-common-dir` that @bobbypierce42 suggested: he reported a machine with 100+ tracked projects where a dozen roots are worktrees of one repo, and round-robin across those would fill the result set with the same symbol at slightly different line numbers, with a per-root cap powerless because each worktree *is* a root and sits under it. The first checkout named wins, and the response reports which roots were collapsed rather than dropping them silently. A root that fails to open is reported inline and the other roots still answer, since one unreadable index should not cost a caller the rest. `tokensave_context` is deliberately **not** federated despite being named in the issue: it returns formatted prose rather than a ranked array, so interleaving has no meaning for it and concatenating per root is a different feature.

### Changed
- **The last handler that loaded every node to build a lookup map now projects it in SQL (#411).** `test_reached_node_ids`, behind `tokensave_complexity`'s `crap` score, used `get_all_nodes()` solely to build a `node_id → file_path` map — the same map `test_risk` needed. It reuses `get_node_paths()`. Every node really is required, since a `Calls` edge can originate anywhere and the question is whether its *source* sits in a test file, but the other twenty-six columns never were. With this, no MCP handler calls `get_all_nodes()`. Output verified byte-identical before and after against this repository's own index.

### Fixed
- **A `recv.method()` call with two equally plausible targets no longer picks one at random (#378).** `resolve_one`'s Strategy 1/1b fall back to matching the trailing segment against *any* node with that bare name when the qualified or typed match fails. Go gates this on the qualifier being a known import (`try_go_selector_match`); TS/JS never got the equivalent, so a call on a receiver whose type is external, unindexed, or a JS builtin bound to whatever local method shared the name. The reporter's follow-up found it cuts both ways: with two candidates tied on every scoring dimension, the tie-break was `if score > best_score` — first-seen wins — so `this.statusBar.dispose()` could be attributed to an unrelated `ProviderManager.dispose`, simultaneously fabricating a caller for one and hiding the real caller of the other, which is how `dead_code` reported live code as dead. The bare-name fallback now returns nothing when the top candidates are tied, rather than guessing: a missing edge degrades an answer, a fabricated one corrupts it, and #346 showed fabricated edges going on to invent cross-language cycles. Measured on this repository the refusal removes 12 call edges and adds none. **The tie-break itself is deliberately unchanged**, and this is the finding worth recording: ties decide roughly 2,834 call edges here, 12.7% of the total, so ordering them deterministically re-points that many in one release — too large to fold into a bug fix, and tracked separately. Diagnosis by @vianbas, including the correction that the ordering effect only reproduces under one file-enumeration order.

### Changed
- **`test_risk` no longer materialises every node to build a lookup map (#411).** It needs a graph-wide `node_id → file_path` map — it walks every edge, and a test in `tests/` calling a function in `src/` is precisely what the tool looks for, so the map cannot be scoped — but it never needed the other twenty-six columns of each row. A new `get_node_paths()` returns the mapping straight from SQL. Its three other passes over the node list concern executable nodes only, so those kinds are now selected in the query rather than filtered out of the whole table three times: on this repository that is 7,556 rows instead of 17,827. Output is byte-identical before and after on this repository's own index, checked rather than assumed.

### Fixed
- **The `auto_track` knob did not reach the `post-checkout` hook, so branches were tracked on every checkout regardless of the setting (#397).** `auto_track` was consulted in exactly one place, `TokenSave::open`. The hook shells out to `tokensave branch add`, and that command never looked at the config, so the knob governed one entry point and not the other — on *fresh* installs as much as on hooks written by an older version. The comment at the gate said "(git-hook path is separate)", so the split was intentional, but it makes #342 Q1's goal of a single runtime knob governing auto-track everywhere unreachable. `branch add` now takes `--if-enabled`, which exits successfully without doing anything when `auto_track` is off (honouring the `TOKENSAVE_AUTO_TRACK` override, the convention `TokenSave::open` already follows), and both the generated hook snippet and `scripts/post-checkout` pass it. The flag is opt-in rather than the default because an explicit `tokensave branch add` typed by a person is a request: silently doing nothing because of a config value they may not know about would be worse than the bug being fixed. The check runs before anything is read or written, so a declined auto-track costs nothing on a checkout. **This reaches new installs only** — the installer skips a `post-checkout` hook that already carries the tokensave marker, so migrating existing hooks remains #342 Q1.

### Changed
- **Six MCP handlers now filter nodes in SQL instead of materialising the whole node table (#410, easy half).** `redundancy`, `module_api`, `unused_imports`, `gini`, `health` and `literal_search` each called `get_all_nodes()` and filtered in Rust, so one tool call on a large project re-triggered a graph-sized allocation — the same peak the sync path has, but on a *read*, recurring per call rather than once per sync. Every one of those filters is a predicate the caller supplied: a path prefix, a set of kinds, a visibility, a minimum line span, a name substring. A new `NodeFilter` pushes them into the query. Measured on this repository (17,594 nodes): `module_api` scoped to `src/` now loads 2,206 rows instead of 17,594, `redundancy` at its default 10-line floor loads 5,018, `unused_imports` loads the 1,611 `use` nodes, a `literal_search` for `config` loads 745, and `health`/`gini` scoped to a path load 9,146. Two escaping details are covered by tests rather than left to review, because both fail silently with wrong results rather than erroring: a path prefix matches only an exact path or one followed by a separator, so `src` never sweeps in `srcfoo`, and `LIKE` metacharacters in a path are escaped, so a directory named `a_b` does not also match `axb`. Each handler's predicate is asserted to return exactly what its in-Rust filter returned. **The two remaining call sites are unchanged** and tracked separately: `test_risk` needs a graph-wide node-id-to-file map because edges point anywhere, and `test_reached_node_ids` is a reachability traversal — neither is a predicate, so neither belongs in this change.

### Added
- **`strict_tree`: refuse `tokensave_*` calls instead of answering from the wrong working tree (#372 §2).** tokensave already detects two ways its index can describe a tree you are not in — a borrowed worktree index (#312) and a branch that drifted under a running server (#400) — but both were advisory: a warning prefixed to an answer the tools produced anyway. The reporter's case for making that refusable is that every tool built on top of tokensave inherits the wrong-tree result with no signal, and an empty result reads as "no such symbol" rather than "wrong tree", so an agent rule saying "always check tokensave before reading files" is silently misled. Setting `strict_tree: true` in `.tokensave/config.json` turns both detections into errors naming the two trees (or two branches) and the remedy. Nothing new is detected; only what a detection *does* changes. It is checked before freshness work and before handler dispatch, so a refused call does no work, and explicitly selected graphs (`graph_root`) are exempt — naming another project's snapshot is a deliberate request for a tree that is not yours. **`tokensave_status` is the one exemption**, since it reports the served root and branch, which is exactly what a refused caller needs; three tools whose names suggest they belong beside it do not, and the reasoning is recorded in the code: `tokensave_config` queries arbitrary TOML/JSON files by key path and says nothing about tokensave's state, while `tokensave_diagnose` and `tokensave_diagnostics` map compiler output onto *graph nodes* and would therefore attribute a real error to a node from another tree — the exact failure strict mode exists to prevent. Off by default, deliberately: sharing one index across a family of worktrees is a legitimate setup, and hard-erroring it uninvited would be a bad surprise.

### Fixed
- **Calls inside ExUnit test bodies never reached the call graph, so `affected` and `test_risk` reported Elixir projects as untested (#387).** `test "..." do ... end`, `describe`, `setup` and `setup_all` are macro invocations rather than language constructs, so tree-sitter sees ordinary `call` nodes carrying a `do_block`. `extract_calls` is only ever reached with a `def`'s node id, so every call in every ExUnit test was attributed to nothing and became no edge. The symptom was not abstract: on an untouched `mix new` project, `tokensave tool affected` returned `affected_tests: []` for a directly tested source file, and `test_risk` reported `has_test: false` with `coverage_pct: 0.0` for a function whose passing test sat in the next file — confidently wrong in the direction that makes someone write a duplicate test, or skip running the test that covers their change. Calls in those blocks are now attributed to the enclosing `defmodule`, matching how #346 attributed calls inside a TypeScript arrow passed as an argument to its enclosing binding; a `describe` block covers the `test` blocks nested in it without double-recording. Verified on the reporter's exact reproduction: `affected_tests` now names the test file, and `test_risk` reports `has_test: true` with `coverage_pct: 100.0`. **`doctest` is deliberately not modelled** — it generates tests from `@doc` examples at compile time, so there is no call expression in the source to attribute, and linking `doctest Foo` to every function in `Foo` would fabricate coverage; a doctest-only function is still reported untested, which is wrong but honestly wrong rather than silently overstated. Reported by @graphitedesk, whose A/B isolation (adding a plain `def` to the same test module and watching `affected` go from 0 to 1) identified the cause precisely.

### Documentation
- **`docs/BRANCHING-USER-GUIDE.md` described a watcher that was removed in 6.1.1, and three of its behavioural claims were wrong (#395).** The "How the watcher handles branches" section was written around the embedded `ProjectWatcher` (`f7f7c9b`, #80), and renaming the actor would not have saved it: it promised that `last_synced_at` advances after each sync, that the sync path re-checks the current branch, and that "you don't need to restart the MCP server after adding a branch". The first two are now true, having been fixed as #399 and #400; the third never was and is now documented as the opposite, since the database a server writes to is resolved once at startup and a `git checkout` underneath it does not change that. The section is rewritten around the on-demand model that actually runs, states plainly that a restart *is* required after a checkout, and shows the drift warning a server now emits. Four further watcher references elsewhere in the file, the non-existent `tokensave mcp` command, and a fallback warning quoted with a `WARNING:` prefix it does not carry are corrected in the same pass. Reported by @vianbas, who verified every claim against both a local build and the published Linux binary.

### Fixed
- **A server kept writing to the branch it started on after a `git checkout`, indexing one branch's files into another's database (#400).** `TokenSave::open` resolves the active branch once and the MCP server holds that handle for its whole life, so a checkout underneath it was invisible: the server carried on serving *and writing to* the original branch, still reporting `active_branch: main`, with no warning on the tool response or on stderr — zero warnings over a 176-second poll across five cooldown windows in the reporter's run. The observable result was `main`'s index containing a file committed only on `feature`, so the index did not merely go stale, it described a tree that had never existed, and every downstream agent rule that treats tokensave as the source of truth inherited that silently. Automatic syncs now detect the drift and refuse rather than write across branches, and each tool response carries a warning naming the served branch and the working tree's branch. Restarting the server remains the way to serve the new branch; re-resolving the handle in place is a larger change and is not attempted here. Single-DB projects are deliberately unaffected: `init` writes branch metadata for the default branch alone, so the guard fires only when the working tree's branch has a database of its own — either tracked, or the default — mirroring the ownership rule in `branch::track_branch_copy`.
- **`hook-pre-tool-use` covered `Glob` in the handler but not in the installed matcher, so the redirect was unreachable (#389).** v7.9.0 extended the hook to path-shaped discovery (#294), and the classifier side shipped: the binary can tell a `Glob` call from a `Grep` call by the fields only `Grep` sends. What did not ship is `Glob` in the `PreToolUse` matcher, the regex of tool names Claude Code uses to decide which calls reach a hook at all — it read `Agent|Grep|Bash`, so a `Glob` call was never routed to tokensave and the branch was dead code. The `find -name` and `fd --extension` halves of #294 were unaffected, since those arrive as `Bash`. The failure was easy to miss because both commands a user would check report success: `doctor` prints `✔ PreToolUse hook installed` after verifying the entry exists, the binary resolves, and the subcommand is right — none of which is the matcher. `expected_hook_matcher` is the single source of truth for both the install path and `doctor`'s drift repair, so widening it to `Agent|Grep|Bash|Glob` also migrates existing installs on their next `install`/`reinstall`, with `doctor` flagging the stale matcher in the meantime. Reported by @xaerogonzo.
- **`last_synced_at` never advanced after a branch was created, while three surfaces rendered it as live freshness (#399).** `BranchMeta::touch_synced` had exactly one caller — the `branch add` arm — so the field recorded when a branch *entry* was created and nothing moved it afterwards, from either the CLI or the MCP server. A real `tokensave sync` that indexed a new file left the value untouched. That matters because `tokensave branch list`, the `tokensave_branch_list` MCP tool, and the `tokensave://branches` resource all present it as staleness (`synced 7d ago`), so a person or an agent asking whether a branch's index was fresh got a confidently wrong answer, and one that grew steadily more wrong the longer the branch lived. The timestamp is now advanced wherever the DB-level `last_sync_at` metadata is written — all three sync-completion points, including the incremental path the MCP server uses most — so the two cannot drift apart. Only the branch actually being served is touched, so a sync on `feature` never claims `master` was synced. The update is best-effort: a sync that indexed correctly does not fail because the metadata file could not be rewritten. Reported by @vianbas.
- **An automatic sync could index a directory from scratch, reaching ~95 GiB of RSS+swap (#396, #393).** The MCP server's startup catch-up sync and its per-`tools/call` staleness check both fed `find_stale_files` straight into a sync with no bound on the result. That is safe only while the index is populated: `find_stale_files` compares the tree against the `files` table, so an index with no files recorded classifies *every* file on disk as stale, and the fallback at `staleness.rs` returns the whole tree outright when the DB read fails. A background task therefore became a full initial index of whatever directory it happened to be pointed at — on a 2.5 TiB home directory that grew to roughly 16.7 GiB RSS plus 78 GiB swap over five hours, saturating zram and hanging `ps` on swapped-out pages; on a project that had never been `init`ed it presented as a `serve` process idling at 98-1370% CPU with no tool call in flight, which reads as a spin loop but was a real extraction running to completion. Automatic syncs now go through `find_stale_files_bounded`, which refuses in two cases: an index with no files recorded (building the first index is `tokensave init`'s job — it is explicit, reports progress, and the user chose the directory), and a stale set larger than `max_auto_sync_files` (default 2000, `0` disables). Both refusals are logged with the reason and the command to run instead, rather than silently serving a stale index. Explicit `tokensave sync` is deliberately unbounded and remains the way to index a large change on purpose; the 30 s cooldown was never a cost bound, only a frequency one, which matters while resolution still materialises the whole node graph at once (#306).

### Added
- **`tokensave_context` output includes a `### Retrieval` section, so a retrieval miss is visible instead of silent.** The section reports the hit count for each searched term, the best post-boost candidate score, and a match tier (`exact`, `strong`, `fts-only`). When query terms match nothing in the index, it lists them (capped at 6, with a `+N more` tail) and tells the agent to re-query with `keywords` synonyms or to switch to `tokensave_search`. When a task yields no searchable terms at all and no entry points, the section still appears and says so. Without this, weak entry points look the same as a sparse graph, and an agent burns follow-up calls on rephrased guesses. It stays the final section of the formatter output, and the context handler's truncation preserves everything from it to the end of the response as a suffix — which also stops a large Code section from silently truncating away `seen_node_ids`, the handle follow-up calls pass back as `exclude_node_ids`. The `exact` tier comes from exact-name and exact-source provenance rather than a score threshold, because multiplicative boosts make the score bands overlap; the score decides only `strong` versus `fts-only`, with the `strong` floor a named constant calibrated to the negated-BM25 band that #314 introduced. The `RetrievalDiagnostics` field on `TaskContext` is additive and `#[serde(default)]`, so stored contexts without it still deserialize.

### Changed
- **Resolution no longer loads the two node columns it never reads (#306, part 1).** `get_all_nodes()` selects every column, and the resulting `Vec<Node>` stays resident for the whole resolution pass, which is the graph-sized peak behind the memory reports. Two of those columns are unbounded TEXT that resolution never touches: an extractor stores a `const`'s entire initializer in `signature` — #362 found one reaching roughly 43 KB — and `docstring` carries full doc comments. `ReferenceResolver` reads only `id`, `kind`, `name`, `qualified_name`, `file_path`, `start_line`, `visibility` and `parent_id`. Both sync paths now use a `get_all_nodes_for_resolution()` that returns `NULL` for those two, keeping column positions identical so the row mapper stays shared. On this repository that omits about 1.25 MB against 2.40 MB of retained node strings across 17.5k nodes — roughly a third of the node string payload — and the saving scales with how large a project's signatures are. **This narrows the peak, it does not bound it:** the resolver borrows from the slice for its lifetime and needs a global name index to resolve cross-file references, so the pass still holds every node at once and the remaining cost stays proportional to node count. Chunking or streaming it needs a resolver redesign, and the analysis handlers that call `get_all_nodes()` independently (health, analysis, redundancy) are untouched. A test asserts the slim and full loads resolve to byte-identical edges, so a future change that starts reading either field fails loudly rather than silently seeing `None`.
- **The Related Symbols section collapses test and fixture files to one line.** A large test file could dominate the section with dozens of `test_*` entries that cost tokens without informing the agent. These files now show as a single count line, labeled `test/fixture files`, with a pointer to `tokensave_callers` for detail. The decision uses the same path classification as ranking (`is_test_path` wraps `path_boost`), so the formatter and the ranker cannot disagree about what a test or fixture file is.
### Fixed
- **The `search_terms` FTS weight is 3.0, not 6.0, now that #359 made the column live.** The column shipped in #316 with a weight described as between `qualified_name` and `name`, but 6.0 is above the 5.0 of `qualified_name`, and no real index ever exercised it: the stale triggers that #358 describes rebuilt `nodes_fts` without the column after every `index_all`. After #359 repaired the triggers, the shape of the column bit. It holds only the inner words of a camelCase name, so the field is one or two tokens, and BM25 length normalization gives a short field at weight 6.0 an outsized score. A PascalCase type that matched one query word could outrank a snake_case function that matched three, because a snake_case name has no inner words and gains nothing from the column. The new weight puts the channel below full-name and qualified-name evidence and above docstring and signature prose, which is where it was always said to sit. On the 12-query retrieval eval from #313 this restores the pre-#359 ranks exactly, and inner-word matching keeps working.
- **`file` and `doc` nodes no longer consume slots in the bounded per-term symbol fetch.** That fetch feeds the symbol candidate pool of the context builder. File and doc nodes are not symbols, and both kinds have their own surfacing channels (the related-files collection, `tokensave_doc`), but they competed for the raw-BM25 slots on common terms. Rerank-time down-weighting cannot repair a pool that was polluted at fetch time. The exclusion matters most on artifact-heavy projects, where #323 now tracks many more non-code rows.
- **A new git worktree, and a clone of a non-default branch, were indexed without their branch being tracked (#391).** The `post-checkout` hook ran `tokensave init` on git's all-zeros previous-HEAD sentinel and `tokensave branch add` in an `elif`, so the two could never both run. But `git worktree add -b feature` and `git clone -b feature` raise that sentinel *and* set git's branch-checkout flag (`$3 == 1`), and they land on a branch that is not the default one: the new index recorded only the default branch, and every MCP query from the worktree warned `branch 'feature' is not tracked — serving from 'main'`. The sentinel arm now runs `init` and then `branch add`, sequentially inside a single background job — sequentially because `branch add` copies the index that `init` creates, and as one job because two independent background jobs would race. `scripts/post-checkout`, the reference hook for manual installs, gains the same behaviour; it had never carried the `branch add` arm added in 7.3.0 at all. Ordinary branch switches, file checkouts, and clones of the default branch are unchanged. **This reaches new installs only:** the installer skips a post-checkout hook that already carries the tokensave marker and the writer never replaces existing content, so an existing hook keeps its old body — migrating those installs is the open policy question in #342 Q1. Until that is answered, an existing install can close the gap with `TOKENSAVE_AUTO_TRACK=1`, or with `"auto_track": true` in that project's `.tokensave/config.json`, on the next command that *opens* the project — a bare `tokensave branch list` does not open it and will not track. Simplest of all is a plain `tokensave branch add` in the new worktree. The section is now fenced with a closing `# tokensave: end auto-init` marker so a migration can replace it in place rather than pattern-matching the shapes that shipped in 6.4.3 and 7.3.0.
- **Stale `node_fingerprints` rows could produce phantom 1.0-similarity results in `tokensave_redundancy` (#380).** Cache validation now hashes the exact indexed node range, cache misses resolve the same line-and-column range in the tree-sitter AST, and cached rows whose stored token count differs from the live body are recomputed.
- **Cargo-driven tests no longer inherit developer-level Git hooks (#381).** A developer who had run `tokensave install` also had a global `core.hooksPath`, so real Git operations inside temporary test repositories executed TokenSave's background `post-commit` and `post-checkout` hooks. Those hooks raced the fixture itself, intermittently taking its sync lock or modifying `.tokensave/` while assertions were running. Cargo now supplies an empty command-scope `core.hooksPath` to the processes Cargo launches, while leaving the developer's global Git configuration unchanged.

### Documentation
- **The docs described an embedded file watcher that was removed in 6.1.1 (#390).** `docs/USER-GUIDE.md` carried a whole "The Embedded File Watcher" section telling readers the MCP server watches the project directory and runs incremental syncs in the background, and offered `watcher_debounce` as the knob to tune it; `UserConfig::watcher_debounce`'s own doc comment said the same. Nothing has read that field since the `notify-debouncer-full` watcher was removed (#80), so `watcher_debounce = "2s"` reads as a two-second freshness guarantee while having no effect whatsoever. The section now describes the model that actually runs: a catch-up sync when the MCP server connects, plus a staleness check at the top of every `tools/call` gated by a 30-second cooldown, with `tokensave sync` as the way to get fresher than that. The field is documented as inert rather than removed — dropping it would silently strip the key from existing `config.toml` files on their next save, and whether to deprecate it is a maintainer call. Two architecture notes in `docs/INDEX-DESIGN.md` and two comments in `src/tokensave/indexing.rs` that still named the watcher as the syncing party now name the MCP server's on-demand sync. Reported by @polarene.
- **Three documents dated the watcher's removal to 6.1.0 instead of 6.1.1, in five places.** The commit that removed it, `f7f7c9b`, is not an ancestor of `v6.1.0`; the first tag containing it is `v6.1.1`, which is also where CHANGELOG.md files the entry. `README.md`, `docs/COMPARABLE-TOOLS.md` (three places) and `docs/TOKENSAVE-VS-TOKENSAVIOR.md` are corrected. Noticed while fixing #390.
- **The bug-report template still offered `Daemon / file watcher` as an affected component.** Both are gone: `ffb5959` removed daemon mode and is first tagged `v6.0.0`, and `f7f7c9b` removed the embedded watcher and is first tagged `v6.1.1` (#80). The option is removed rather than renamed; the form keeps its `Other` option, and the field has no `default:` and no `validations: required`, so nothing else about it changes. Noticed while fixing #390.
- **SECURITY.md described the database as structural metadata only, which understated what it retains (#377).** The policy named `read_cache` as the sole place source text is kept, but `executable_body_fts` is declared without `content=''`, so FTS5 retains the original text of every column in `executable_body_fts_content` — including `body`, which holds complete function bodies. The enumeration above the sentence said "FTS5 search index", which does not tell a reader that full source is retained. Corrected in three places: the storage list, the sentence that follows it, and the Best Practices note, which likewise mentioned only symbol names and `read_cache`. No storage change is involved: the bodies are load-bearing for `tokensave_context`, and a contentless table would return NULL for `SELECT node_id, body`. The "Supported Versions" table, still listing 6.x as current, is updated to 7.x in the same pass.

## [7.9.0] - 2026-08-08

### Added
- **Verilog / SystemVerilog extraction, with an `instantiates` edge for the design hierarchy (#344).** A project containing tracked `.v` files could `init` and `sync` successfully while none of its RTL reached the graph — the files were not listed and no module was searchable. `.v`, `.vh`, `.sv`, and `.svh` are now handled by one SystemVerilog grammar (`lang-systemverilog`, in the `full` tier), extracting modules, interfaces, programs, packages, classes, functions, tasks, parameters/localparams, typedefs, package imports, and class inheritance. Module and interface instantiation becomes a new `EdgeKind::Instantiates` rather than being folded into `calls`: an instantiation is structural hierarchy, not invocation, and conflating them would put RTL hierarchy into callers/callees, impact, and dead-code results for every other language. Resolution of an `instantiates` reference is restricted to module and interface targets, so an instantiated vendor cell that is not in the index produces no edge at all instead of binding to whatever else shares its name. Internal nets and variables are deliberately not indexed — an RTL design declares them by the thousand, and they would swamp the graph without answering the questions the hierarchy is consulted for.
- **`tokensave_imports`: module-level import dependencies, cycles, and cut simulation (#334).** `tokensave_circular` answers dependency questions at the *file* level using `calls`/`uses` edges, which is the wrong unit and the wrong edge for planning a decomposition — one `from mod_b import X` produces zero `calls` edges or fifty depending on how the name is used, so counting calls says nothing about how many statements a refactor must touch. The new tool groups files into modules (configurable `depth`), reports which modules are mutually reachable, enumerates every import statement holding a given dependency together with its file and line, and — via `simulate_removal_from`/`simulate_removal_to` — recomputes the components as if one dependency were gone, which is what distinguishes a cut that breaks a cycle from one that leaves everything still mutually reachable. Imports written inside a function body are flagged `lazy`, since a lazy import usually exists *because* of a cycle and costs far less to remove than a module-level one. No new edge kind was required: extractors already emit a `Use` node per import statement carrying the imported path, line, and source text, and the resolver already binds it to its target, so the import graph is a projection of data the index already held.

- **`hook-pre-tool-use` now covers path-shaped discovery: the `Glob` tool, `find -name`/`-iname`, and `fd -e`/`--extension`/`-g` (#294).** The hook handled Agent dispatch and symbol-shaped Grep/Bash searches, but not discovery by path, so a separate Python or PS1 shim had to replicate policy for those — the two-implementation problem, where whichever layer is stricter or staler wins. Matching calls over code extensions are redirected to `tokensave_files`, which is only now a safe target: before #323 it was populated solely by symbol extraction, so redirecting `find -name "*.feature"` would have traded a working command for an empty result. The redirect is deliberately narrow, since it is a claim that the graph can answer the question: non-code extensions, mixed searches, search roots outside the index, `fd`'s default regex form, and any `find` predicate that changes what the command *does* (`-exec`, `-delete`, `-mtime`) all pass through untouched, while flags that only narrow results (`-type`, `-maxdepth`, `fd -t`) do not defeat it. `Glob` is told apart from `Grep` — both carry `pattern` — by the fields only `Grep` sends, so a content search is never misread as a path search. `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK=1` opts out of the new paths exactly as it does the existing ones.
- **Non-code artifacts are now indexed by path, so `tokensave_files` can find them (#323).** The `files` table was populated only by the symbol-extraction pass, so `.feature` specs, fixtures, schemas, and docs were absent from it: `tokensave_files(pattern="**/*.feature")` returned nothing, the pattern was valid and simply had nothing to match, and the `find` fallback is blocked by the hook — leaving "where are the .feature files?" with no answer at all. Files with the configured `artifact_extensions` (`.feature`, `.json`, `.yaml`, `.yml`, `.sql`, `.toml`, `.proto`, `.graphql`, `.md` by default) are now tracked with a content hash and stat, so incremental sync adds, re-hashes, and removes them like any other file. They ride the existing scan, so exclude globs, `.gitignore`, the symlink-cycle prune, and the size limit all apply unchanged. Artifacts are never parsed and contribute no symbols or edges: a new `files.kind` column (schema v16) keeps them distinguishable from a source file that merely yielded no symbols, `tokensave_files` takes a `kind` filter and labels them `artifact` rather than `0 symbols`, and source-text scanners (`unsafe_patterns`, `field_sites`, exact-phrase context) skip them. An extension already owned by a language extractor is ignored in `artifact_extensions`, so configuring one cannot stop a language being parsed.
- **Sessions are now told which neighbouring projects they can reach with `graph_root` (#375).** The selector shipped in #363 could already query any sibling checkout, but nothing revealed that a sibling existed, so a session spanning two repos read an empty result as "no such symbol" — and with the shell fallback blocked by the hook, had no way left to find it (#322). Initialized projects sitting directly beside the served root are now named in the `initialize` instructions, in `tokensave_status`, and — most usefully — in empty `tokensave_search` and `tokensave_context` results, which is the moment the information is needed. Only immediate siblings qualify, so an unrelated checkout elsewhere on disk is never suggested, and at most five are listed so a crowded scratch directory cannot flood the response. Nothing is opened, indexed, or queried on their behalf; the list is a hint, and querying a sibling still requires an explicit `graph_root`.
- **Semantic MCP queries can explicitly select another initialized local project with `graph_root` and an optional tracked `graph_branch` (#363).** Selection is per call and read-only: it never initializes, syncs, migrates, auto-tracks, edits, or changes the server's default graph. Selected responses carry canonical root/branch provenance and graph-namespaced node IDs for safe follow-up traversal, while selected calls remain outside savings accounting.
- **`init` and `sync` now report tracked source files that were skipped because no extractor handles their extension (#345).** A repository whose only supported files are documentation could finish `init` with an apparently complete index, or report `sync done - 0 added, 0 modified, 0 removed`, without any hint that its actual source files were omitted, which made an unsupported language indistinguishable from an ignore rule, a size limit, a parse failure, or an empty project. Both commands now print a compact, aggregated line (`Skipped 1 tracked file (unsupported extension): .vhd (1)`), listing at most the five largest extension groups and rolling the rest into an `and N more extension(s)` tail, so a large repository never prints thousands of paths. The summary is grouped by extension and filters known binary and asset extensions, matching what `--verbose` and `sync --doctor` already showed in detail; `IndexResult` now carries `skipped_extensions` alongside `SyncResult`. Exit status and successful sync behavior are unchanged.
- **Ruby projects now have graph-parity coverage that compares complete node and edge records after incremental sync with a clean full index.** This catches count-preserving drift where one relationship disappears and another incorrect relationship takes its place.
- **`tokensave_context` takes an optional `max_code_lines` parameter to cap each code snippet by line count (#324).** Code snippets already have an approximately 1500-byte size limit and include a `tokensave_body` handle when truncated; the new parameter complements that protection by letting callers request a deliberately short, predictable prefix such as the first 10 or 20 lines of each relevant symbol. Both limits apply, with the tighter one winning, and truncation still occurs on a line boundary. Snippets below both limits are returned unchanged. The line cap defaults to unlimited and explicit values are clamped to 1–1000.
- **`tokensave cost`/`monitor` now accounts for Factory Droid local `.factory/sessions/**/*.settings.json` snapshots.** Exclusive per-session `tokenUsage` key names — `inputTokens`, `outputTokens`, `cacheCreationTokens`, `cacheReadTokens`, `thinkingTokens` (`thinkingTokens` is folded into normalised output; not exposed separately) — plus observed `factoryCredits` are imported and exposed via `cost --by-agent`. Credits are reported as a separate unit and never converted to USD. Cumulative sessions are bucketed by `providerLockTimestamp` as the copy-stable session start; `droid` `/limits` remains the authoritative monthly balance. When no Droid source exists, existing `cost` output and the original two-line monitor remain unchanged.

- **Calls made outside a function body now count as uses, cutting the `tokensave_dead_code` false-positive rate (#346, parts 2c/2d).** The reporter measured 97% false positives on a Go package tree and 3 of 3 on a Vue/Pinia frontend, noting the TypeScript cases were "the simplest possible case (same file, same closure/module scope, direct call)". They were, and the cause was that both extractors attributed call sites only to an enclosing *function* node. A call anywhere else was recorded against nothing at all. Three concrete gaps are closed. A statement-level call in TypeScript (`registerHandlers();` at module top level, a side effect that runs at import time) is now attributed to the file node, which is the enclosing scope the graph actually has. Calls inside a `const` initializer are attributed to the binding — the Pinia shape from the report, `export const useStore = defineStore("x", () => { loadFromStorage() })`, where the arrow passed as an argument gets no graph node of its own, so every helper it called looked uncalled. And in Go, a function used as a value rather than called (`var SandboxSuffixFunc = randomSandboxSuffix`) now emits a reference; the existing scan only looked for call expressions, and a bare identifier initializer contains none. That last one is deliberately scoped to a var spec's own initializer rather than every `expression_list`: emitting a reference for each identifier in every assignment and return would link unrelated same-named symbols across packages, which is the over-linking that fabricates cycles in part 1 of the same report. On the issue's reproductions the TypeScript fixture goes from 3 flagged (all false) to 0, and the Go fixture from 2 flagged (1 false) to 1 — the one genuinely dead function. The same-package test-caller case from the report (`reset()` called from `broker_test.go`) already resolved correctly and now has a regression test so it stays that way. Every fixture carries a genuinely dead function as a control, since the point is to remove false positives without blinding the tool.
- **`tokensave_search` now has an exact-phrase channel, and no longer returns invalid JSON for a symbol with a huge signature (#362 follow-up).** The reporter noted that `tokensave search "Waiting for status"` did not return the localization catalog that literally contains the phrase, and the earlier `tokensave_context` fix did not address it. The cause is separate from the BM25 effect: `search` sorts by kind tier first — deliberately, so a `use` re-export can never outrank a real definition — and a catalog is a `const`, so it could not beat a function however well it scored. A multi-word query now also runs an FTS *phrase* match, and those hits are promoted ahead of the tier sort, before truncation, so the one symbol whose text actually contains the phrase leads the results. Single-word queries are untouched: the phrase helper returns nothing for them, so ordinary name lookup ranks exactly as before. Fixing this exposed a second defect. The catalog's signature is roughly 44 KB, and the tool serialized it verbatim; that blew the 15,000-character response budget and the truncation cut mid-string, so `tokensave_search` handed its caller **malformed JSON** — reproducibly, whenever a result carried a large value initializer. Entry-point signatures were already bounded in the context formatter by the change above; that helper is now shared and applied to the search, node, and related tool payloads too. The same query goes from 15,033 bytes of unparseable output to 2,613 bytes of valid JSON.
- **`tokensave_context` no longer loses an exact quoted phrase to generic semantic matches (#362).** A context request quoting a UI string — "Diagnose why the dashboard still shows Waiting for status" with `"Waiting for status"` as a keyword — returned nothing but decoy `loadDashboardStatusNNN` functions, omitting both the localization catalog holding the phrase and the component rendering it, 5 runs out of 5 on the reporter's fixture. The catalog was indexed and FTS did match it: the extractor stores a const's whole initializer as its signature, so the 800-entry catalog is one 43 KB field, and BM25's length normalization scored that unique, exact hit at −0.07 against −5.58 for hundreds of short decoys matching only the generic words `dashboard` and `status`. The single most specific piece of evidence in the query ranked 26th. The machinery to handle this already existed — `find_exact_source_candidates` greps source, attributes each hit to its innermost enclosing node, and scores it above every FTS channel — but it was unreachable here, because `exact_source_terms` splits on whitespace and keeps only `::`-qualified tokens, so a multi-word phrase was discarded before the scan ever ran. Phrases now reach that scan: any caller keyword containing inner whitespace, plus any double-quoted span in the task text, is matched verbatim. Single words are deliberately excluded (FTS already covers them, and scanning source for one common word matches everywhere), phrases must be at least 8 characters and two words, and the list is capped at 8 so the scan stays bounded. On the reporter's fixture the catalog goes from absent to the first entry point. Two things this does *not* fix, both confirmed while testing: `tokensave_search` still ranks the catalog below short semantic matches for the same BM25 reason, and the component cannot be reached by graph expansion because the TypeScript extractor emits no edge from a function body to a module-level const it reads — `translate` → `MESSAGES` is simply missing from the graph, so the `Banner` → `translate` → `MESSAGES` path is broken at the last hop.
- **An entry point's signature is bounded to one line of at most 200 characters.** The extractor stores a value binding's entire initializer as its signature, so surfacing a localization catalog or a generated lookup table printed tens of kilobytes into the context response — on the #362 fixture, a single entry point rendered 15 KB, more than the source file it came from, which is a poor trade in a tool whose purpose is spending fewer tokens. The first line still identifies the symbol and the code block below it carries content under its own existing limits; truncation is marked with an ellipsis and happens on a character boundary. The same fixture now renders 3.2 KB.
- **`report_savings` turns off the savings narration that costs output tokens (#356).** tokensave exists to cut *input* tokens, but it asks the agent to tell the user what it saved — via the `tokensave_metrics:` line appended to every accounted tool result and a sentence in the MCP `instructions` pointing at that line. The agent obliges on nearly every turn, and those are *output* tokens, the more expensive kind, spent on a message that wouldn't otherwise exist; as the report puts it, that "largely offsets the benefits". The new per-project `report_savings` config field (default `true`, so nothing changes for anyone who doesn't ask) removes both when set to `false`: no metrics line, and the `instructions` stop asking for the report. `TOKENSAVE_REPORT_SAVINGS` overrides it per-run using the same falsey spellings as `TOKENSAVE_AUTO_TRACK` (`0`, `false`, `no`, `off`, empty), whose ad-hoc match is now the shared `config::env_bool_override` both settings call. `tokensave install` also stops writing the reporting rule into the Vibe prompt file; that runs at global scope with no project config in reach, so it reads the env var and bakes the choice into the file it regenerates. Measurement is deliberately outside the gate: the per-call ledger row, the persisted counter, and the monitor entry are all still written, so `tokensave gain`, `list`, `status` and `monitor` report exactly what they did before — the setting governs what the *model* is shown and asked to say, not what tokensave knows.
- **`tokensave init` on an already-initialized project is covered by regression tests (#372 §1).** The report described observing exit 0 with a silent-looking message in an interactive session, which cost real debugging time when a caller trusted `init`'s exit code as proof a rebuild had happened. That could not be reproduced: the already-initialized branch calls `exit(1)` with no TTY-dependent path, and the binary was confirmed to exit 1 both with stdin closed and under a pty, so the reported behaviour was a version difference rather than a live defect. No behaviour changed; `tests/init_already_initialized_test.rs` now pins it down through the real binary across the explicit-path, argument-less, and relative-`.` invocations, and asserts the refusal still names `sync --force`, so the guarantee cannot drift back.

### Changed
- **The structural call-graph tools `tokensave_impact` and `tokensave_callees` are now `anthropic/alwaysLoad` (#333).** Their schemas load into the model context up front instead of sitting behind a `ToolSearch` round-trip. Measured usage across 26 days and five repositories showed the three previously-eager tools (`context`/`search`/`status`) taking 96.7% of calls while the call-graph tools — the ones with the clearest advantage over grep-and-read for "who calls this?" / "what breaks if I change this?" — went essentially unused, because a tool the model has to already suspect exists is a tool it rarely goes looking for. The always-loaded set grows from three tools to five; the schemas are small and the prompt prefix is cached, so the up-front cost is negligible. `callers` and `affected` are deliberately left deferred for now, pending a measured usage shift.
- **Symbol FTS now stems at the index (`porter unicode61`), and camelCase inner words are indexed at insert time (schema v14).** Inflected prose in a `tokensave_context` task — "candidates", "truncation", "generates" — previously matched nothing, because the unicode61 tokenizer is exact-token and the identifiers are `candidate`/`truncate`/`generate` (#264 called out the missing `generates` → `generate` normalization). The porter tokenizer makes that class of match work for any suffix at query time instead of patching common cases query-side. A new `search_terms` column carries the words a tokenizer cannot derive on its own — the inner words of a camelCase identifier (`updateCloudClient` → `Cloud Client`), computed once at insert by `text::search_terms` and weighted between `qualified_name` and `name` in `bm25()`; it stays empty for snake_case names, which unicode61 already splits on `_`, so the column adds nothing for codebases like this one and closes the #202 class (spaced natural-language query vs. camelCase TSX symbol) for the TS/Java/Go side of the language table. Migration v14 adds the column, backfills it, and rebuilds `nodes_fts` with the new tokenizer — all derived data, so the worst case is a re-index; nothing user-authored is touched.

### Removed
- **Both query-side stemming workarounds are deleted, subsumed by the porter tokenizer.** The trailing-`s` strip from #264 and `generate_stem_variants`' hand-rolled suffix table existed only because the index didn't stem. Porter covers every conflation the suffix table targeted — verified directly against FTS5 (authentication ↔ authenticator ↔ authenticate, configuration ↔ configure, serializer ↔ serialization, truncation ↔ truncate, validator ↔ validated); the one jump neither makes is resolution → resolve, so nothing is lost. On a 12-query retrieval eval the entry points are identical with the workarounds removed.

### Fixed
- **The Python extractor now indexes imports written inside function bodies.** Only module-level `import` / `from … import` statements were visited, so a deferred import produced no `Use` node at all — invisible to `tokensave_unused_imports` and to the new import graph, and precisely the case that matters most there, since a function-body import usually exists to break a cycle. Nested definitions are still skipped by this walk, so their imports stay attributed to the function that actually contains them rather than the enclosing one.
- **Project paths are canonicalised at the global-DB boundary, so one directory can no longer occupy several rows (#372).** Every key written to or read from `~/.tokensave/global.db` — `projects.path` and `savings_ledger.project_path` alike — now goes through `global_db::normalize_project_key`, which resolves the path on disk (collapsing `.`, `./`, `../name`, symlinks and trailing separators), strips the `\\?\` and `\\?\UNC\` verbatim prefixes that `canonicalize` returns on Windows, and upper-cases a drive letter so `d:\Work\ProjectA` and `D:\Work\ProjectA` land on the same row. A path that cannot be resolved — a project directory that has since been deleted, or a synthetic path in a test — falls back to a lexical absolute form, so the key stays stable rather than collapsing onto the working directory. Two symptoms motivated this. A literal `.` was registered as a project name: `tokensave list -a` showed a row named `.` carrying a real size but no recoverable identity, and `doctor`'s stale-purge could never remove it, because `.` resolves to some real indexed directory from wherever `doctor` happens to run and so is never considered stale. And on Windows, case-variant drive letters accumulated token counts independently (14.4M on `d:\Work\ProjectA` against 12.2M on `D:\Work\ProjectA` for one confirmed-identical directory), which both under-reports `tokensave gain` per project and double-counts disk usage in `list -a`'s total. The relative-argument entry below fixed the resolver that produced the `.` row in the first place; this change makes the storage layer itself refuse the ambiguity, which is what also covers the drive-letter case, paths arriving from the MCP server, and any future caller that reaches the DB with an unnormalised path. A one-time migration, gated on a new `meta` table so it costs nothing after the first open, rewrites existing rows: duplicate spellings are merged by summing their `tokens_saved`, ledger rows are re-pointed at the canonical key so a project's recorded history follows it, and rows keyed by a relative path are dropped, since they name no recoverable directory and would otherwise stay unattributable and unpurgeable forever. The migration is best-effort in the same way as the rest of `GlobalDb`: on failure the flag stays unset and the next open retries. This addresses sections 3 and 4 of #372; the worktree-mismatch strict mode (section 2) and `init`'s already-initialized exit signal (section 1) are unchanged.
- **Schema migration v15 restores secondary indexes and FTS triggers lost by an interrupted bulk load, even when the `.tokensave/dirty` sentinel is absent (#358).** `begin_bulk_load` drops every edge, node, and unresolved-ref index plus the FTS and trait-dispatch sync triggers; `end_bulk_load` recreates them — but only if it runs. The #318 fix added sentinel-based healing in `TokenSave::open`, yet that path only fires when the dirty sentinel exists. If the sentinel was never written, was already cleaned up by a prior `open()`, or was removed manually, the DB stayed permanently unindexed: `PRAGMA quick_check` passes (the B-tree is intact), `sync` pinned a core at ~100% CPU doing full-table scans of the `edges` table (46M rows / 4.6 GB in the reported case) on every `delete_nodes_by_file` call, and the MCP server silently served an unindexed graph. Migration v15 recreates the indexes and triggers that bulk load drops with `CREATE … IF NOT EXISTS`, each gated on its base table so the repair is a safe no-op on a partial schema (#359) — instant on a healthy DB, a full repair on a damaged one.
- **`end_bulk_load` now recreates the v14-era FTS triggers that include the `search_terms` column, not the pre-v14 bodies that omitted it (#358).** After every `index_all` on a v14 DB, `end_bulk_load` dropped the FTS sync triggers and recreated the old bodies without `search_terms`, silently downgrading the FTS index so `tokensave_search` could no longer match camelCase inner words (`updateCloudClient` → `Cloud Client`) or porter-stemmed inflections on rows inserted or updated after a full reindex. The trigger bodies now match `create_schema` and `migrate_v14`.
- **Relative path arguments such as `tokensave init .` now work instead of aborting.** Every command that takes an optional project path resolved it with `PathBuf::from` and handed the value straight through, so a relative argument stayed relative even though both resolver functions document an absolute result. In a debug build `init` and `sync --force` then hit `debug_assert!(project_path.is_absolute())` and panicked with `init_and_index: project_path must be absolute`; in a release build the assertion is compiled out and the relative path is persisted instead, so `tokensave init .` registered the project as `.` in the global database while any later argument-less invocation registered the same project again under its absolute path, splitting that project's savings totals across two rows and pooling every project initialized that way into the same `.` row. Passing no argument was unaffected, because that branch already used `current_dir()`, which is why `tokensave init` worked while `tokensave init .` did not. A non-absolute argument is now joined to the working directory and normalized lexically, so `.` and `..` collapse to a root the rest of the codebase can compare and store, while absolute arguments are returned untouched and an existing project root keeps its exact spelling, symlinks included. On Windows this also covers a rooted but driveless argument such as `\tmp\project`, which `Path::is_absolute` rejects and which is now resolved against the current drive the way Windows itself resolves it. Normalization is lexical rather than `canonicalize` so that a path that does not exist yet still resolves, and so that Windows never returns a `\\?\` verbatim prefix into stored roots or user-facing messages.
- **Ruby calls with statically provable class/module receivers now resolve to singleton methods.** Constant receivers follow Ruby lexical nesting and respect shadowing assignments; `self` calls resolve from singleton methods and directly from class/module bodies, including targets defined inside `class << self`, while blocks that change `self` are excluded from the enclosing body. Ambiguous or dynamic receiver shapes remain unresolved, and the first incremental sync upgrades Ruby files from an existing index so unchanged singleton definitions participate immediately.
- **Database test suites no longer leak a temp directory per test on Windows (#367).** The setup helpers returned `(Database, TempDir)` — or `(TokenSave, TempDir)`, or `(Arc<McpServer>, TempDir)` — and every test bound the pair as `let (db, _dir) = setup().await`. Rust drops the bindings of a `let` in reverse order of declaration, so `_dir` went first and `remove_dir_all` ran on the directory while the connection still held `test.db` open. POSIX unlinks an open file happily, which is why this was invisible on Linux and macOS; Windows refuses, the removal fails, and `TempDir`'s destructor discards the error by design. Measured on Windows, `db_query_test` left 75 of its 76 directories behind on every single run, each holding its `test.db`. Repeated runs accumulate: the batches used to measure the crash in the companion change stranded 48,245 directories totalling 20.25 GB on the system drive before the leak was noticed, and filled it. A CI runner that is already tight on disk is exactly where that reads as an unrelated "no space left on device" flake. Every affected helper now returns the `TempDir` first and the call sites bind `let (_dir, db)`, which puts the database's drop ahead of the directory's; `resolution_test`, `integration_test`, and `memory_test` already ordered it that way and are untouched, and `corruption_test`'s three-element helper needed the same treatment as `migration_test`'s. Each suite re-run on Windows afterwards leaked nothing; so did a sweep of all 111 test binaries run twice, though that sweep had the companion libsql fix applied as well. This is a distinct defect from the double close above — the batches show the same leak with and without that fix — but it is the same class of bug: a resource lifetime that only POSIX forgives.
- **Every database connection was being closed twice, a use-after-free that surfaces on Windows as the intermittent `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` of #367 and #308. Fixed for CI and for builds from a checkout; binaries installed from crates.io are not covered until libsql itself ships the fix, for the reason given at the end of this entry.** libsql's `Connection::disconnect` is not idempotent: `LibsqlConnection`'s `Drop` calls it, and then the inner `Connection` field's own `Drop` calls it again. The `Arc::get_mut(drop_ref)` guard only proves unique ownership — true on both passes — and `raw` is left dangling after the first `sqlite3_close_v2`, so the second call reads an already-freed `sqlite3` object. Instrumenting the driver made the ratio exact rather than inferred: one run of `db_query_test` performs 76 `sqlite3_open_v2` calls and 152 `sqlite3_close_v2` calls, 76 returning `SQLITE_OK` and 76 returning `SQLITE_MISUSE`, and the same 1:2 ratio holds on the `GlobalDb` path. What was measured is that the second close returns `SQLITE_MISUSE` on Linux rather than faulting: the freed page is still mapped and its state check fails harmlessly. That the same read faults on Windows once the block has been recycled or decommitted is the explanation for the platform asymmetry, not a second measurement. On Windows the block can be recycled or decommitted first, and the read then either faults immediately or closes a garbage handle and corrupts the heap, which detonates later, at process exit, after every test has already reported `ok`. That is the signature #367 describes, and it explains why the same bytes pass on one run and fail on the next. This is not CI-only: any `tokensave.exe` closing a database on Windows runs the same double free. Batching the affected binary on a Windows machine puts a number on it: `db_query_test` crashed 29 times in 500 runs (5.8%) on the unpatched build, every one of them exit code `0xC0000005` after all 76 tests had reported `ok`, and 0 times in 500 runs with the fix applied — a rate that would produce a crash with probability 1 − 10⁻¹³ had it been unchanged. The other two suites named in the reports behave identically: `accounting_test`, the first one #367 cites, crashes 10 times in 500, and `resolution_test`, the suite #308 was originally filed against, 13 times in 500 — both clean afterwards, so the same cause is present in all three unrelated binaries and in both issues. That is not proof it was the only cause of every past occurrence, but nothing else has reproduced since. The batches also retire the standing theories: serialising the harness with `--test-threads=1` does not hide the crash but roughly triples it (47 in 300), so parallel test execution is not what drives it — which is as far as that experiment reaches, since it removes concurrency between tests and not every runtime teardown path; quadrupling `RUST_MIN_STACK` to 32 MB leaves the rate unchanged (12 in 300), so no amount of stack is relevant; and a suite that opens no database is clean in 500 runs. Across four binaries and 2100 runs with the fix in place there was no crash of any kind, and a sweep of every one of the workspace's 111 test binaries, run twice on Windows, came back with no access violation and no other failure. That sweep ran with this fix and the drop-order fix of the companion change applied together, so it bounds the two of them jointly rather than this one alone. The fix is one line — null `raw` after closing so the second pass is a no-op — and it is applied in `vendor/libsql`, a copy of the published 0.9.30 crate wired in through `[patch.crates-io]`, carrying upstream's MIT licence and dropping its examples, tests, benches, test assets, lockfile and contributor notes, with `[lints]` and `[workspace]` tables added to its manifest and the one-line change below, because the bug is still present upstream in both 0.9.30 and 0.10.0-pre.4 (libsql#2251, with the equivalent fix waiting in libsql#2261). `tests/vendored_libsql_test.rs` fails if the vendored copy loses the patch or drifts from the requested version, so the patch cannot be dropped silently; the vendored crate and the `[patch]` entry both go away once an upstream release carries the fix. One limit is worth stating plainly: `cargo package` strips `[patch.crates-io]` and leaves `vendor/libsql` out of the published archive, so this covers CI and anyone building from a checkout, while binaries produced by `cargo install tokensave` keep the upstream double free until libsql itself ships the fix. Neither #181's `RUST_MIN_STACK` nor #337's `/STACK:8388608` could have addressed this — both target stack exhaustion, and this is a heap lifetime bug.
- **Ruby call references now preserve explicit receivers instead of resolving by method name alone.** Calls such as `User.find`, `worker.perform`, and `user&.profile` remain receiver-qualified and no longer create edges to unrelated same-named methods.
- **Graph selector and untracked-branch errors now tell the caller how to recover.** Selecting the project the MCP server already serves said only that the root matched, and agents that pass `graph_root` explicitly were observed retrying the same failing call; the message now says to omit `graph_root` (and `graph_branch`, when present) to query the currently served graph, and states that selecting a different branch of the served project is not supported instead of leaving the branch intent unaddressed. `graph_branch` without `graph_root` carries the same guidance, and an untracked explicit branch now names the remedy (`tokensave branch add '<branch>'`, shell-quoted) in both the selected-graph and served-graph open paths, matching the fallback warnings that already did. The `graph_root`/`graph_branch` schema descriptions shown in `tools/list` now say to omit them for the served project and that `graph_root` must name a different one.
- **Tracking a branch no longer copies a truncated snapshot of the parent DB when the parent has an uncheckpointed WAL (#292).** Both `tokensave branch add` and the transparent auto-track path copied only the `.db` file, but a long-lived MCP server keeps the parent DB open in WAL mode, so committed rows — routinely the most recent sync's worth — can sit exclusively in the `-wal` sidecar at copy time. The copy then opens clean (`quick_check` passes; the B-tree is intact) while silently missing that data, which is how a branch DB ends up answering `tokensave_context`/`tokensave_search` from checkpointed tables while `tokensave_files` reports 0 files: the symptom reported in #292 and previously misattributed to branch fallback. A shared `copy_branch_db` helper now snapshots the parent with `VACUUM INTO`, which writes a transactionally consistent copy — main file plus WAL contents — under the source connection's read snapshot, correct even while a concurrent connection is mid-commit; sequencing a checkpoint and two separate file copies can never make that guarantee, since a checkpoint plus WAL reset landing between the copies would mix a pre-checkpoint `.db` with a post-reset `-wal`. Only when the snapshot itself fails (the parent is not a readable SQLite file, or an exotic locking failure) does the helper fall back to a best-effort `wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` followed by a plain file copy, bringing the `-wal` sidecar along if the checkpoint could not empty it so SQLite recovers it on the new DB's first open. The already-tracked, default-branch, and no-metadata no-op paths are unchanged.
- **`branch-meta.json` is now written atomically (temp file + rename), so a concurrent or interrupted branch-track can no longer leave it torn.** `save_branch_meta` did a plain `std::fs::write`, so two `track_branch_copy` calls racing on the same repo, or a crash mid-write, could truncate or corrupt the file that maps every tracked branch to its DB; a corrupt file silently falls back to single-DB mode, so every non-default branch would then serve the ancestor's stale index. The JSON is now written to a temporary file in the same directory, flushed, and renamed over the target, which is atomic on POSIX and Windows.
- **Two git branches whose names sanitize to the same filename no longer share one branch DB and silently overwrite each other's index.** `sanitize_branch_name` maps `/`, `\\`, `:`, `.`, spaces and more to `_` and collapses runs, so `feature/foo`, `feature_foo` and `feature.foo` all became `feature_foo.db`; neither `tokensave branch add` nor the transparent auto-track path checked whether that filename was already claimed by a different branch before copying and recording it, so tracking the second branch clobbered the first branch's DB. A new `unique_branch_db_file` helper, used by both paths, keeps the readable stem when it is free and appends a short stable hash of the full branch name only on collision, so the branch-to-DB mapping is deterministic and collision-free. Existing non-colliding names are unchanged.
- **An interrupted bulk load no longer leaves the graph with duplicate edges and no indexes, and can no longer poison every later sync (#318).** `begin_bulk_load` drops `idx_edges_unique`, the index the `INSERT OR IGNORE` edge writers rely on to dedupe, and only `end_bulk_load` recreates it. If a load was interrupted in between (for example the `database table is locked` failure of #320), the graph was left un-indexed and possibly duplicated; `PRAGMA quick_check` passes on that state because the B-tree is intact, so it was served as-is and every subsequent sync piled on more duplicates — one reported database reached 914k duplicate rows over ~28k real edge tuples with no edge indexes. Worse, once duplicates existed, `end_bulk_load`'s `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX` could never succeed, so finalization failed permanently. `end_bulk_load` now collapses duplicate edge tuples (keeping the lowest `rowid` per `source, target, kind, COALESCE(line, -1)`) before recreating the unique index, and `TokenSave::open` detects a missing `idx_edges_unique` on a dirty reopen and re-runs finalization — deduping and rebuilding the dropped indexes — instead of serving the broken graph. This is a targeted repair, not a full re-index.
- **Windows test binaries no longer crash intermittently with `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` (0xc0000005), a main-thread stack overflow the existing `RUST_MIN_STACK` could not prevent (#308).** The crash appeared across unrelated test binaries with no assertion or panic, only on Windows, while Linux and macOS stayed green — the signature of running out of stack on the one thread `RUST_MIN_STACK` does not resize. That variable enlarges the threads Rust spawns, but the main thread's stack is fixed at link time and defaults to about 1 MB on MSVC against roughly 8 MB on Linux and macOS. A deep recursive AST walk (`extract_call_sites_recursive`), or resolver work that rayon's work-stealing runs on the calling thread, therefore overflowed on Windows alone and only when the deep frame happened to land on the small main-thread stack, which is why it was intermittent. The Windows MSVC target now links with `/STACK:8388608`, raising the main-thread reserve to 8 MB to match `RUST_MIN_STACK` and the other platforms; the reserve is address space committed lazily, so there is no runtime cost, and non-Windows and Windows GNU builds are untouched.
- **The PreToolUse guardrails now activate anywhere inside an indexed project, and resolve that project from the working directory the harness reports.** The hook required `.tokensave/tokensave.db` to sit in the hook process's own working directory, while `serve`, `sync`, and `status` all discover the project by walking ancestors. An agent started in a subdirectory of an indexed repository (a package inside a monorepo, for example) therefore had working tokensave MCP tools and a silently disabled guardrail, so every symbol-shaped `grep` there passed through with no savings and no explanation. Discovery now walks ancestors like the rest of the codebase, and Claude Code, Kiro, and Factory Droid events are resolved from their own top-level `cwd` with the hook process's directory as the fallback, which also makes Kiro's `preToolUse` hook agree with the `postToolUse` sync hook that already read `cwd`. A reported `cwd` is only honored when it is an absolute path to an existing directory, since the value is agent-supplied and a relative or stale one would start the ancestor walk somewhere nobody meant; every other value falls back to the hook process's directory. The `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK` opt-out, the pattern and target classifiers, and Droid's built-in `explorer` task guard are unchanged. `HookEnv::cwd_has_tokensave_db` is renamed to `HookEnv::in_tokensave_project` now that the flag no longer describes one directory.

- **The indexing walk no longer follows a directory symlink back onto the project root or one of its ancestors, which made `init`/`sync` unbounded and pinned a core in `serve` (#327).** Every Wine prefix ships `dosdevices/z: -> /`, so a project that contains one (or whose root is `$HOME`) sent the walker across the whole filesystem: the reporter measured 683,707 crossings in 40 s and two leaked `serve` processes that burned 20.5 h and 14.4 h of CPU. Both walkers and the gitignore fallback probe now prune a directory symlink whose canonical target is the canonical project root itself or one of the root's ancestors; such a link can only re-expose paths the walk already covers, plus everything else on the disk. Symlinks into disjoint trees are unaffected and are still followed and indexed, so the external-source-tree layout stays supported; the guard is checked before the hidden-directory, `include`, and manifest rules, so nothing can re-enable an escaping link, and the walk root itself is exempt so a project opened through a symlinked path still scans. Rows indexed through such a link before this fix are removed by the next full `sync`. Deliberately unchanged: a symlink into a large *disjoint* tree is still followed (use `exclude` or `--skip-folder`), and non-symlink filesystem cycles such as bind mounts are outside the walkers' view.
- **`tokensave upgrade` no longer offers to kill its own launcher, and no longer proceeds if it cannot signal every process it was asked to kill.** Candidate selection now excludes this process and every reachable parent in the process tree (e.g. a Scoop shim or shell that launched `tokensave`), so it can't be listed as a killable "other" process; unrelated `tokensave` MCP servers are still offered.
