If `--kill`/an interactive `y` cannot signal every listed process, the upgrade now aborts instead of continuing, telling the user to stop them manually and retry.
- **`tokensave serve` now understands the `file://` URIs that MCP clients send in `roots`, which had made the roots-based project discovery dead on Windows (#331).** When neither the working directory nor the global database identifies a project, `serve` peeks at the `initialize` request and resolves the workspace from its `roots` array — the path that covers a VS Code multi-folder workspace, or any client launched from outside the project. It stripped a bare `file://` prefix, which is correct only on Unix: a Windows root arrives as `file:///D:/Dev/app`, leaving `/D:/Dev/app`, a path that matches no registered project and that `discover_project_root` cannot walk, so the fallback silently found nothing and the server exited telling the user to run `tokensave init` in a project that was already indexed. Percent-encoding was ignored as well, so any path containing a space (`file:///C:/My%20Project`) failed on every platform. The URI is now decoded — on bytes, so a percent-encoded multi-byte character reassembles correctly, and a decode that does not form valid UTF-8 (a lone `%C3`, a `%FF`, a truncated sequence) rejects the root instead of collapsing distinct malformed inputs into the same U+FFFD replacement path — and the leading slash is dropped only when a drive letter follows it, leaving a genuine Unix root like `/d/dev/app` alone. Registered projects are compared canonically rather than literally, so the same directory spelled `D:\Dev\app` and `D:/Dev/app` is recognized as one. Non-`file:` schemes and non-local authorities are skipped instead of being interpreted as relative paths. This is the inverse of `agents::kiro::file_resource_uri`, which already produced exactly this URI form.
- **The response to a peeked-and-replayed `initialize` request is now newline-terminated, so the roots fallback completes the MCP handshake with a live client (#331).** `serve`'s last-resort discovery consumes the first stdin line to read `initialize.roots` and replays it through `handle_and_write`, which wrote the serialized response without the trailing `\n` that the main `run()` loop appends. MCP frames stdio messages by line, so a connected client sat waiting for the response line to finish and the handshake never completed — the response was flushed but unterminated, and only an EOF (the closing pipe delivering the partial line) ever released it. Piped one-shot harnesses see exactly that EOF, and the test suite's channel transport frames by message rather than by line, so neither noticed; before the URI fix above the fallback never resolved a project on Windows at all, which is why the two bugs could only meet in a live client session. Found while verifying #331 end-to-end on a real Windows MCP client.
- **`annotates` edges no longer bind an annotation usage to another usage of the same annotation, including itself.** The resolver allowed an `Annotates` reference to target a sibling `AnnotationUsage` or `Decorator` node, and every extractor names that reference after the annotation itself, so any `@override`/`#[test]`/`@Override`-style usage could resolve straight to another usage instead of staying unresolved — a phantom edge affecting 11 language extractors. The resolver now never produces `annotates` edges at all; the attachment edge extractors already emit directly at the usage site is the only one, matching what every consumer reads. Schema migration v14 removes any phantom edges already stored in existing projects' databases automatically on next open, no resync required.
- **Dart calls made inside closures and local functions are no longer dropped.** `extract_call_sites` skipped `function_expression`/`lambda_expression` outright instead of attributing their calls to the enclosing named symbol, so calls inside Flutter callbacks (`onPressed:`, `builder:`, `setState(…)`, `.map(…)`) and local functions were invisible to `tokensave_callers`, `tokensave_impact`, and `tokensave_dead_code` — a helper reached only from a callback looked dead. Same fix as TypeScript #209 for nested arrows.
- **A project indexed by the CLI no longer force-reindexes on the first MCP tool call of every session, and a failed forced reindex no longer leaves the project with an empty index (#320).** `last_indexed_version` was written only by the MCP server's `run_version_reindex`, so every project created by `tokensave init` kept the `String::new()` default. `bump_kind("", running)` classifies an unparseable `old` as `BumpKind::Major`, so the first `tools/call` of each session logged `major upgrade or schema change detected (indexed by "", running <version>) — forcing project reindex…` and ran a full reindex — on a project that was already current. `index_all` opened that reindex with `db.clear()` *before* `db.begin_bulk_load()`, so when `begin_bulk_load` lost the table lock to a second tokensave process (a common setup: VS Code, VS Code Insiders, and one or more CLI agents all registered against the same project) the rows were already gone, the reindex returned early, and the marker was never advanced. The server treats that failure as non-fatal and keeps serving, so every subsequent tool call answered out of a gutted graph — observed as `file_count: 1` on a 259-file project — and the same destructive cycle repeated on the next start, since the marker still said `""`. A full index now records the running version, so the redundant reindex never starts; and `begin_bulk_load` runs before `clear`, so a lock failure aborts with the previous index intact instead of destroying it. Clearing after the FTS triggers are dropped also avoids firing a per-row FTS delete trigger for every node.

## [7.8.1] - 2026-07-27

### Added
- **Ruby `def`s nested inside a `do…end`/`{…}` block, in expression position, or inside a method body are now extracted** — `ActiveSupport::Concern`'s `included do ... end`, `class_eval do ... end`, `class_methods do ... end`, an arbitrary RSpec-style `describe … do`, a plain `[1].each do … end`, and even a `def install; [1].each { def m; end }; end` all previously dropped their contents entirely. This is a correction, not new syntax support: `visit_node`'s `"do_block" | "block"` dispatch arm (and the `"body_statement"` arm it recurses into) has existed since the extractor's original commit, but was unreachable — a block is always the `block` field of a `call` node, and nothing ever called `visit_node` on it, since the `call` arm dispatched only to the two directive handlers rather than recursing generically. A new `visit_block_body` supplies that entry point, dispatching on a new `BlockScope` classifier that models Ruby's actual default-definee rule rather than treating every block as a scope boundary: an ordinary block (`each`, `tap`, `describe`, `configure`, …) does not change the definee at all, so it's traversed unconditionally — an explicit receiver is irrelevant, and a `private` before or inside the block flows through it in both directions, matching Ruby 3.4.5. Only `class_eval`/`module_eval`/`class_exec`/`module_exec` (plus `Concern`'s `included`/`prepended`, which are `base.class_eval(&block)`) and `instance_eval`/`instance_exec` (plus `Concern`'s `class_methods`, approximated as `SingletonScope::Enclosing`) actually push a fresh definee, and only those get the previous treatment: skipped when the call has an explicit receiver (`Foo.class_eval do … end` defines methods on `Foo`, which can't be resolved here, so its block is left untraversed rather than mis-attached to the enclosing scope), and a visibility frame that starts public and doesn't leak in either direction, mirroring how class/module bodies already behave. No new scope is pushed in any case, so a top-level `describe … do` block's defs are extracted as `Function`s, same as a bare top-level `def`. `visit_block_body` was still only reached when the block-bearing call was itself a statement, so a block written in **expression position** — an assignment RHS (`CALLBACK = proc do … end`), a call argument (`foo(list.map { … })`), a call receiver (`list.map { … }.first`), or a bare lambda literal (`-> { … }`) — still dropped its `def`s. A new `visit_expression_blocks` (mirroring `extract_call_sites`'s descent and definition-scope skip-list) closes that gap, wired in wherever an expression can appear in statement position. `proc`/lambda bodies only run when called, so these `def`s are indexed even though they're not guaranteed to execute — same documented approximation the extractor already makes for `include Foo if enabled?` and an `if false` branch — since the captured definee (the enclosing class or module) is still known statically. The explicit-receiver skip for `class_eval`/`instance_eval`/etc. (`Other.class_eval { … }` must not attribute its methods to the enclosing scope) is unchanged and still applies through the new descent. A third reachability gap sat one level further in: **method bodies** were never traversed for definitions at all, regardless of blocks — `def install; [1].each { def generated; end }; end` never produced `C::generated`, even though `C.new.install` genuinely defines that instance method. Neither `visit_method` nor `visit_singleton_method` opens a definition scope (no `node_stack`/`class_depth`/`singleton_scope` change), so a nested `def` simply follows Ruby's cref once the body is visited: it lands on the *instance* side even when the enclosing method is itself a singleton method (`def self.install; [1].each { def m; end }; end` defines an instance method, not a class method — confirmed against Ruby 3.4.7), and on the *class* side when the enclosing method is inside `class << self`. A method body does get its own **fresh public visibility frame**, unlike a block body, which inherits one: a class-body `private` does not reach a `def` written inside a method body, and a bare `private` inside one cannot leak back out past `end` — the one place block and method bodies deliberately differ, both directions probed against Ruby 3.4.7. This also means the `include`/`prepend`/`extend`/visibility directive dispatch described below now reaches into method bodies too — correct and needed for the singleton/class-method cases (`def self.setup; class_eval { include Mixin; def m; end; private; def n; end }; end`), and only over-permissive for a receiverless directive written directly inside a plain instance-method body, where `self` is the instance rather than the class, so that particular form would raise at runtime and can't occur in code that actually runs. Three follow-on classification bugs in this same block-scope machinery, all confirmed against Ruby 3.4.7: first, `visit_block_body`'s explicit-receiver skip was too broad — right for an unresolvable receiver (`Other.class_eval`), but wrong when the receiver names the *current definee* (`self.class_eval { def x; end }` and `C.class_eval { def y; end }` inside `class C` both define `C#x`/`C#y`, and were previously dropped, since a receiverless call **is** `self.<call>` and must classify identically in every scope). A new `classify_block_receiver`/`BlockReceiver` distinguishes `Current` (no receiver, or `self`) from `EnclosingConstant` (the constant naming the *innermost* enclosing class/module — `matches_enclosing_scope_path` already had exactly the right suffix-at-innermost-only semantics, reused as-is) from `Unresolvable` (everything else, including a constant naming an outer-but-not-innermost scope, which `qualified_prefix`/`parent_node_id` cannot address). The two are not interchangeable inside `class << self`: `self.class_eval`/`class_eval` there still define a *class* method (ambient `singleton_scope` stays `Enclosing`), but `C.class_eval` names the class itself and must define an *instance* method, so `visit_block_body` now forces `singleton_scope` to `Outside` specifically for a `ReceiverBody` block with an `EnclosingConstant` receiver. Second, `classify_block_scope`'s catch-all wrongly assumed every unlisted method preserves the definee, which is false for the class-factory family — `Class.new`, `Module.new`, `Struct.new`, and `Data.define` all put the block's defs on the brand-new anonymous object they create and leak nothing to the enclosing scope (`K = Class.new do; def x; end; end` previously fabricated a top-level `Function` named `x` that Ruby never defines anywhere reachable, corrupting `callers`/`impact`/`dead_code`). A new `BlockScope::Opaque` variant, selected by receiver *and* method name together (never `"new"` alone — `class C; Foo.new { def x; end }; end` is an ordinary block and genuinely defines `C#x`), makes `visit_block_body` skip these outright instead of mis-attributing them. Synthesizing a class scope for `K = Class.new do … end` is a real feature (`visit_assignment_for_const` currently emits a `Const` node, not a class node) left as a follow-up, not attempted here. Third, `classify_block_scope` keyed the `Concern` hooks (`included`/`prepended`/`class_methods`) on method name alone, ignoring the receiver — so ordinary Ruby that happens to reuse one of those names (`registry.included { def generated; end }`) classified as `ReceiverBody`/`ReceiverSingleton`, `registry` then classified as `Unresolvable`, and the explicit-receiver skip discarded the whole block. Unlike `class_eval`/`module_eval`/`instance_eval`/their `*_exec` forms — real `Module` methods that retarget the definee for *any* receiver — `included`/`prepended`/`class_methods` carry no intrinsic scope-changing semantics in Ruby; only `Concern`'s own receiverless DSL form (`Current`/`EnclosingConstant`) does, and on any other receiver they're ordinary calls whose block inherits the enclosing cref, exactly like `each`/`tap`. The three Concern arms now additionally gate on the resolved `BlockReceiver`, falling through to `Inherit` on `Unresolvable` instead of `ReceiverBody`/`ReceiverSingleton`, so the block is traversed with the definee unchanged rather than dropped. `class_methods` on a non-DSL receiver still lands on the *instance* side, not the singleton side, matching the DSL form's own probed behavior. A fourth classification bug, adjacent but distinct: nothing verified the enclosing module was actually an `ActiveSupport::Concern` at all, so a receiverless call named `included`/`prepended`/`class_methods` was treated as scope-changing DSL even with no Concern in sight — `class C; def self.class_methods(&block); block.call; end; class_methods { def generated; end }; end` wrongly registered `generated` as a singleton method (Ruby 3.4.7: it's an instance method), and a receiverless `included` under `private` wrongly got a fresh public frame instead of inheriting the surrounding `private` (also probed against Ruby 3.4.7). A receiverless call to any of these three names raises without something making it work (`Module#included`/`#prepended` require a mandatory arg; `class_methods` is otherwise undefined), so a new `ExtractionState.in_concern_scope` flag now requires positive evidence before the DSL classification applies: either an `extend ActiveSupport::Concern` seen earlier in the same module body (`visit_mixin_directive`), or a receiverless `concern`/`concerning` block (Rails' `Module#concern`/`#concerning` build a module pre-extended by Concern with no visible `extend`, confirmed end-to-end against activesupport 8.1.3), reset on entry to each nested class/module so it can't leak from an outer scope. `class_eval`/`module_eval`/`instance_eval`/their `*_exec` forms are untouched — they're real `Module` methods and need no such evidence. `in_concern_scope` is additionally scoped by what `self` denotes in the current body, mirroring `visibility_mode`/`singleton_scope`: reset on entry to a plain `def` or `class << self` body (where a receiverless `extend ActiveSupport::Concern` would target the instance or the singleton class, not the enclosing module, and `class_methods` raises there), but inherited into a `def self.x` singleton-method body (where `self` is still the module and the DSL genuinely works) and always restored on exit, so evidence picked up while statically walking any of these three body kinds can't leak into sibling statements. `self` inside a plain instance-method body is treated as an unnamed instance rather than the enclosing class, so a nested `def self.foo`, `class << self`, or `instance_eval` there is no longer misattributed as a class method; and `concern`/`concerning` blocks are only recognized when their topic is a literal naming a valid constant, not by call name alone.

### Fixed
- **Stable preferences and machine-local state no longer regress when multiple tokensave processes save concurrently.** Routine state persistence leaves `config.toml` untouched unless stable preferences changed, while locked baseline merging preserves pending-token deltas, cache freshness, intentional version downgrades, and concurrent agent changes; a deleted `state.toml` is safely recreated without resurrecting stale values.
- **Grep guardrails now recognize the absolute (or `~`-rooted) spelling of the indexed project root, which used to slip through while `.` was redirected.** Agents commonly write the repository target in full (`rg -n handle_request /Users/me/projects/app`, or `path: "/Users/me/projects/app"`), and the classifier only matched `.`, `./`, an omitted target, a recognized code directory basename, or a supported source extension; for an absolute directory it compared just the last path component, so any repository whose folder name is not a known code directory produced no savings. The hook now also treats a target whose canonical path equals the canonical project root as the same whole-project search as `.`. Deliberately exact, and never string-prefix based: a sibling such as `<root>-old`, a parent, and every path inside the project (`<root>/docs`) keep their current behavior, an explicit `type` filter or file glob still wins over the path, and an unknown root, unknown home directory, or any path that cannot be canonicalized fails open. Only absolute (or `~`-rooted) spellings reach the new comparison, so a relative detour such as `../..` keeps its previous handling and the two extra `canonicalize` calls stay off the common path.
- **The Bash grep guardrail's shell tokenizer no longer strips backslashes on Windows, which had silently disabled the absolute-root redirect added above.** `shell_split` treated `\` as a POSIX escape character in unquoted tokens, so a Windows absolute target such as `C:\Users\me\project` was mangled to `C:Usersmeproject` before the classifier ever saw it; `canonicalize` then failed, the hook returned allow, and the test for an absolute project root panicked with an EOF-on-empty-JSON. On Windows `\` is now a literal path separator in the unquoted context (matching cmd.exe and PowerShell semantics), while the double-quote behavior — `\` escapes only before `"`, `\`, `$`, and `` ` `` — is unchanged on every platform. Unix builds are untouched.

### Removed
- **`tokensave_context` no longer advertises a call budget.** Its description carried a `CALL BUDGET: N calls maximum for this project` line, with N picked from a node-count size tier (3 for the smallest projects, 10 for the largest). The intent was to stop an agent re-querying the same question with slightly different wording, but the cap fired on the wrong cases: a genuinely multi-part question in a small repo hit "3 calls maximum" long before it was answered, and the instruction to "synthesise from what you have — do not exceed the budget" pushed the agent to guess rather than to look. It was never enforced server-side either — nothing rejected call N+1 — so its only effect was talking a well-behaved agent out of the tool that exists to be used. It also cost the very tokens it was meant to save: because the budget was derived from the node count, its value moved whenever the project grew across a tier, and tool descriptions live inside the system prompt — so a re-tiered project rewrote the cached prefix and forced the client to discard and rebuild its prompt cache. That is the same failure #260 fixed by removing the node count from this text; the budget was the remaining graph-derived value in it. The description is now a plain statement of what the tool returns, and `tools/list` serves the same static definitions as every other tool, so the whole size-tier lookup and the graph-stats query behind it are gone.

## [7.8.0] - 2026-07-26

### Added
- **`tokensave upgrade` now offers to kill the tokensave processes that are still running, and takes `--kill` to do it without asking.** The processes in question are almost always MCP servers a running agent spawned: they hold the old binary's DB handles open and keep serving code that no longer matches the on-disk index after the upgrade, which is why the guide has always told you to restart your agent afterwards. `upgrade` now lists every other tokensave process by pid and command line and asks once, right after it has confirmed a newer version exists — so an already-up-to-date run never prompts, and neither does a machine with nothing else running. `--kill` skips the question for scripted upgrades. A non-interactive run without `--kill` prints the list, warns, and continues rather than blocking on a prompt nobody can answer. On Homebrew installs the check runs before delegating to `brew upgrade`, since Homebrew decides for itself whether there is anything to do. A process that exits between being listed and being signalled counts as killed, not as a failure.
- **The `tokensave` entry plank writes now carries `primaryTools`, capping what plank inlines into its system prompt.** Plank expands the full JSON schema of every advertised MCP tool into its system prompt; with all ~80 tokensave tools that is roughly 66 KB of schema, enough prompt bloat that plank's model starts emitting malformed tool calls — which surfaces as an apparent connection failure rather than as the prompt-budget problem it is. `install --agent plank` now writes a six-tool primary set (`tokensave_context`, `tokensave_search`, `tokensave_read`, `tokensave_callers`, `tokensave_callees`, `tokensave_status`); every other tool remains reachable through plank's compact one-line-per-tool directory and its `mcp_describe`, so nothing is lost, only deferred. A user's own non-empty `primaryTools` list is preserved across reinstalls and upgrades; an absent key, a non-array value, or an empty array is replaced with the default, since an empty array is indistinguishable from a botched edit. `tokensave doctor` reports the tool count and warns when an entry written before this change has no `primaryTools`.

## [7.7.0] - 2026-07-26

### Added
- **Plank coding agent integration.** `tokensave install --agent plank` registers the MCP server in Plank's config — `~/.plank/.mcp.json` for the user scope, `./.mcp.json` with `--local` for the project scope, matching Plank's own two-layer MCP precedence. Install and uninstall read-modify-write only the `tokensave` server entry, so other configured servers and unrelated top-level keys survive both; uninstall deletes the file only when tokensave was its sole entry. Brings the supported-agent count to 20.
- **Companion Markdown documentation is now indexed and retrievable, so an agent can read a short summary instead of a 3000-line class (#154, phase 1).** Two conventions are discovered, deliberately both: **sidecar** files (`BigClass.cs` alongside `BigClass.readme.md`) need zero configuration and travel with the code in review, and a **docs directory** (`tokensave-docs/` by default, relocatable via the new `docs_dir` config field, or disabled by setting it empty) holds docs whose YAML front matter carries an `applies_to` glob list — one instructions file covering a whole family of files, which is the `**/*.es8.cs` vs `**/*.es7.cs` case from the issue. Both resolve to the same internal mapping and are stored as `Doc` nodes with `Documents` edges to the covered `File` nodes, so coverage is queryable through the existing graph rather than a parallel subsystem. A new `tokensave_doc` tool returns a file's documentation with its content, the full set of files that doc covers, and a drift signal; and `tokensave_entities` now reports `has_doc`/`doc_path` so an agent sees a summary exists *before* deciding to read the file. Drift is detected by comparing the doc's last commit against its covered files' — code committed after the doc sets `doc_stale: true`, and without git history the field is `null` (unknown) rather than a confident "not stale". Docs covering no files are dropped rather than indexed, since a glob can legitimately go empty when files are removed. Front-matter parsing is hand-rolled (one key holding a list of strings does not justify a YAML dependency) and degrades to "covers nothing" on anything it cannot read, never a hard error; it tolerates CRLF, a BOM, and both inline and block sequence forms. Section-level anchors — line ranges or `m:MethodName` symbol references — are deliberately **not** in this phase: whole-doc granularity ships first so the anchor design can be settled against real usage, per the reporter's own read that phase 1 covers most of the need.
- **`tokensave_context` entry points now show the first line of the symbol's docstring** beneath the signature. The one-line summary often answers the query outright, saving the follow-up code fetch; entries without a docstring are unchanged.
- **Ruby `include`/`prepend`/`extend` of a named module now emit a resolvable `Implements` edge from the including class or module.** These mixin keywords weren't handled at all — `include Comparable` parsed as an ordinary `call` node and was silently dropped — so a module's methods never showed up as part of the including class's API, a module included by many classes showed zero inbound relationships, and `dead_code`/`impact`/`module_api`/`type_hierarchy` saw no Ruby composition whatsoever (`ActiveSupport::Concern`, `Comparable`, `Enumerable`, every `app/models/concerns/*` file). The extractor now recognizes a receiverless `include`/`prepend`/`extend` call inside a class or module body and emits one `Implements` ref per `constant`/`scope_resolution` argument (`include A, B` emits two; `include ActiveSupport::Concern` resolves the scoped name); a dynamic argument (`extend self`, `include some_variable`), an explicit receiver (`mod.include Bar`), or a top-level `include` (which mixes into `Object`, with no class/module node to attach to) are left unhandled rather than fabricating a ref. Resolving these refs required a new arm in the resolver's `kind_compatible` gate: a Ruby `Implements` ref (`lang_from_path(&uref.file_path) == "ruby"`) now resolves *exclusively* to `NodeKind::Module` — not the shared `Trait | Interface | InterfaceType | Class | InnerClass | AbstractMethod | SealedClass | Annotation | TypeAlias` list every other `Implements`/`Extends`/`DerivesMacro` ref uses. The rule is exclusive rather than additive because Ruby itself forbids mixing in a class (`include SomeClass` raises `TypeError: wrong argument type Class (expected Module)`) — an additive rule would let a same-named `class Foo`/`module Foo` pair resolve to whichever sorts first in the resolver's internal suffix index, and would also (incorrectly) let `Extends`/`DerivesMacro` target a `Module`, neither of which Ruby ever emits. `extend`'s singleton-method semantics and `include`/`prepend`'s instance-method semantics both produce the same `Implements` edge kind for now — a distinct edge kind is a separate follow-up. `attr_*`, `alias`/`alias_method`, `define_method`, `module_function`, and `ActiveSupport::Concern`'s `included do ... end` block remain unaddressed. A mixin (or any `def`/`class`/`module`/constant) nested inside a conditional, `case`, loop, or `begin`/`rescue` in a class or module body — e.g. `include Foo if enabled?`, an `if RUBY_VERSION > "3" ... else ... end` guard, or `begin; include M; rescue LoadError; end` — is extracted exactly as if written directly in that body, since the extractor now recurses through these statement containers instead of dropping their contents; method bodies are still not traversed this way. A `private`/`protected`/`public` visibility directive nested the same way now also applies past the end of its container, matching Ruby's own runtime scoping (a `private` inside `if legacy?; end` switches the mode for everything that follows the `end`).
- **`TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_SERVER=true` is the canonical per-project MCP server opt-out.** The server still exits cleanly before project initialization, and a source-level regression test now requires future tokensave-owned environment variables to use the `TOKENSAVE_` namespace.

### Changed
- **Truncated `tokensave_context` code blocks now end with a follow-up handle instead of a dead-end ellipsis.** A snippet cut at `max_code_block_size` closes with `... [truncated — full body: tokensave_body node_id=<id>]`, so the caller can fetch the remainder in one call rather than re-deriving which symbol the fragment belonged to.

### Deprecated
- **`DISABLE_TOKENSAVE=true` remains supported as a compatibility alias with no scheduled removal.** Existing configurations continue to disable the MCP server unchanged; new configurations should use `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_SERVER`.

### Fixed
- **Global agent setup now warns before a Cargo build-output path becomes a persistent command.** Running `install`, `reinstall`, or an automatic agent resync from `target/debug` or `target/release` still preserves the exact invoked binary, but now names the path and explains that `cargo clean` or worktree removal will break the configured hooks and MCP servers. Stable installs, project-local installs, and the Homebrew Cellar-path rewrite are unchanged.
- **The reference resolver no longer holds three copies of the node graph, cutting the sync/serve memory peak by roughly 2 KB per indexed node (#253).** `ReferenceResolver::from_nodes` built its lookup caches by cloning: every node was cloned once into `name_cache` and a second time into `qualified_name_cache`, the suffix index allocated a *full copy of the qualified name per `::` segment* (a path like `a::b::c::d` cost four), and `known_names` cloned every key of all three maps on top of that. Because the caller's `Vec<Node>` stays live alongside them, peak RSS was several multiples of the graph itself — the reported 8-10 GiB kills on a ~4.3M-node project, which the OOM killer took down along with the rest of the desktop session. This was never a leak, which is why it looked like one only under `sync`: it is a transient peak, entered by any sync that indexes at least one file, including the startup catch-up sync, and released the moment resolution finishes. The caches now borrow from the caller's slice (`HashMap<&str, Vec<&Node>>`, `HashMap<&str, Vec<&str>>`, `HashSet<&str>`), so construction allocates the index structure and nothing else. Resolution results are byte-identical — candidate ordering still follows node order, `find_best_match` scores the same set, and the whole suite passes unchanged. Measured on this repo (403 files, 15,595 nodes): peak RSS 353.5 MB → 322 MB. Remaining follow-ups, not addressed here: the caller's full `Vec<Node>` is still materialised at once, and several analysis handlers independently call `get_all_nodes()`.
- **`resolve_db_for_branch` no longer opens a hardcoded `tokensave.db` when falling back to the default branch (#300).** Both the detached-HEAD arm and the last-resort arm reported serving `meta.default_branch` but ignored that branch's actual `db_file` in `branch-meta.json`. On a project whose default branch is mapped to `branches/<name>.db` — the normal layout once the default branch has been through `branch add` — both arms opened a wrong or nonexistent DB while claiming to serve the default branch, so queries silently ran against an empty or stale index. Both now resolve through `branch::resolve_branch_db_path`, keeping `tokensave.db` only as the genuine last resort when branch metadata has no entry for the default branch. Same class of bug as ac3db6c, which fixed the equivalent hardcoding in the `pr_context` handler. The conventional layout, exact-match, and no-metadata single-DB paths are unchanged and now covered by regression tests.
- **Prose documentation files no longer outrank code in `tokensave_search` and `tokensave_context` results.** Markdown headings are indexed as `Module` nodes with `Pub` visibility, so the kind (1.2×) and visibility (1.5×) boosts handed doc headings a 1.8× multiplier over neutral code that merely shares vocabulary with the query — a search for a config symbol could put `CHANGELOG.md` above the defining module. `path_rank_multiplier` now applies a 0.3× factor to doc extensions (`md`, `markdown`, `mdx`, `rst`, `adoc`), between the existing fixture (0.1×) and test (0.4×) factors, and shared by both ranking paths. Vendor classification still wins for docs inside vendored trees. Proportional, not a filter: an exact heading match still surfaces, and when docs are the deliberate target (a `path_include: ["docs"]` filter, or a docs-only repo) every surviving candidate shares the same factor, so their relative order is unchanged.
- **Literal search (`tokensave_search` with `literal: true`) now honors `.tokensave/queryignore` like the ranked path.** The literal branch returned before the ranked path's queryignore filter ran, so paths a project had explicitly suppressed (#116) still appeared in literal matches. The indexed file list is now filtered with the same patterns before scanning — the same gap-closure shape as the `path_include`/`path_exclude` literal-mode fix in #258.

## [7.6.2] - 2026-07-25

### Added
- **CUDA/HIP `.cu`/`.cuh` files are now indexed (`lang-cuda`, `full` tier).** These extensions had no registered extractor, so every `.cu`/`.cuh` file indexed as zero symbols regardless of size. CUDA is a C++ superset, so a new extractor delegates to `CppExtractor` — the same pattern as Metal, no new grammar dependency. The one exception is `<<<grid, block>>>` kernel-launch syntax, which derails tree-sitter-cpp badly enough to drop the *enclosing function* entirely; every `<<<...>>>` span is now blanked to same-length spaces before parsing, the same technique the GLSL/Godot extractor uses for its own dialect syntax.

### Fixed
- **`tokensave_pr_context` no longer fails with `git error: cannot resolve 'main'` on repositories whose default branch isn't named `main`.** The handler hardcoded `main` as the fallback base ref instead of reading the indexed `default_branch` from `branch-meta.json`, which is what `handle_branch_diff` already does — so on a `master`-default repo (or any other name) every call that fell back to the default base errored out. The fallback now resolves from branch metadata, with `main` kept only as a last resort when no metadata is present.
- **The `tokensave init` warning for a directory outside a git repository no longer claims that `.gitignore` filtering is off, because it isn't (#283).** The scanner registers `.gitignore` as a custom ignore filename (`add_custom_ignore_filename` in `scan_files_with_gitignore`), so ignore files are read directly rather than through git repository discovery and their patterns apply with or without a `.git` directory — the reporter's TFVC-managed project was already getting the filtering the warning told them they had lost. What a non-repo tree actually loses is the exclude sources Git itself supplies: `.git/info/exclude` and the user's global excludes file. The warning is now written from the project's real state: with a root `.gitignore` it says those patterns still apply and names what doesn't, and without one it keeps the large-tree caution (#174) that motivated the warning, since nothing then constrains the walk, and points at the two remedies — add a `.gitignore`, which works without git, or set `exclude` globs in `.tokensave/config.json`. A regression test indexes a non-git fixture and asserts a `.gitignore`'d directory is skipped while its siblings are indexed, so the wording and the scanner can't drift apart again.
- **`tokensave init` no longer claims to have excluded `.tokensave` from git when it did nothing, and no longer reads stdin when it isn't a terminal (#288).** In a directory with no git repository, `init` printed `warning: could not locate .git/info/exclude` immediately followed by `Added .tokensave/ to .git/info/exclude (local, untracked)`: `add_to_git_info_exclude` and `add_to_gitignore` warn and bail on every failure path but returned `()`, so the caller announced success unconditionally — a failed write, an unlocatable git dir, or a missing parent directory all reported the same. Both helpers now return whether the entry is present (an already-present entry counts as success, since the desired end state holds) and the caller reports only what actually happened. Two adjacent problems in the same block are fixed alongside: the prompt is skipped entirely outside a git working tree, where neither answer has any effect, and it is now guarded by `stdin().is_terminal()` like the equivalent prompt in `main.rs`, so a scripted or agent-driven `init` no longer silently consumes a line of the caller's stdin and takes it for an answer.
- **Grep guardrails now honor an explicit file glob before a broad search path.** Claude and Factory Droid can send both a root such as `path: "."` and a narrower `glob` / `glob_pattern`; the shared classifier previously discarded the glob whenever `path` was present, so a legitimate Markdown search such as `**/*.md` was hard-blocked as a code search. Explicit code-only globs still redirect, mixed code/non-code globs pass through, unclassifiable globs fall back to the existing path rule, and an explicit `type` filter remains highest priority.
- **C++ declarations wrapped in `extern "C"` are indexed again, instead of silently vanishing with zero symbols and no error.** Both the single-declaration and block forms parse as a `linkage_specification` node, which had no case in the extractor's dispatch — a pattern near-universal in C/C++ interop headers. `linkage_specification` (and its block-form `declaration_list` body) now dispatch through the existing generic child-walk; namespaces already unwrap their own `declaration_list` before dispatch, so this can't double-visit them.
- **godot-cpp `_bind_methods` bindings now link the C++ method they expose to GDScript, so bound methods stop showing 0 callers / 0 impact and the binding boilerplate stops being reported dead (#269).** A Godot 4 GDExtension registers C++ methods for GDScript inside `_bind_methods` via `ClassDB::bind_method(D_METHOD("register_item", "cfg"), &MyRegistry::register_item)` (and the `bind_static_method` variant), and GDScript then calls them as ordinary methods (`MyRegistry.get_singleton().register_item({...})`). The C++ call-site walker only recorded each `call_expression`'s *callee* — `ClassDB::bind_method`, `D_METHOD` — none of which resolve to an indexed node, and it never followed the pointer-to-member *argument* `&MyRegistry::register_item`, so the bound method received no incoming edge from its own registration: `callers` came back empty, `impact` reported one node and zero edges, and `dead_code` flagged the method. `_bind_methods` itself, being invoked by the engine's `ClassDB` machinery rather than by any indexed code, was likewise reported dead. The C++ extractor now recognizes a `bind_method` / `bind_static_method` call (matched on the callee's final `::` segment, so both the bare and `ClassDB::`-qualified spellings are covered) and emits a `Calls` reference from the enclosing function to each pointer-to-member argument's qualified method name (`MyRegistry::register_item`), which the resolver links through its existing qualified-name match; only `&Class::method` pointer expressions are followed, so `DEFVAL(...)` default-argument expressions in the same call are not mistaken for bound methods. This alone clears the bound-method false positive (the method now has an incoming edge from `_bind_methods`) and gives the `_bind_methods → method` edge; no resolver change is needed for the GDScript side, because a GDScript call already resolves to the C++ method by its short name (the `D_METHOD` exposed name matches the method name by godot-cpp convention) and the singleton accessor becomes live naturally once `get_singleton()` resolves. Separately, `dead_code` now exempts any node named `_bind_methods` as an engine-contract entry point, alongside `main` and `test*`. Scope: name-based linking only, matching the reporter's proposal — no type inference, and `ADD_SIGNAL`, `bind_vararg_method`, and property (`ClassDB::add_property`) bindings are intentionally left out.
- **`.gdshader` node signatures keep the original Godot hint/qualifier text instead of the blanked copy fed to the parser (#272).** The Godot-dialect support shipped in 7.6.1 (#270, #275) rewrites `: hint_…` clauses and `global`/`instance` qualifiers into same-length spaces so the vanilla GLSL grammar can parse the file — but it then extracted every node's signature and excerpt from that rewritten copy, so `uniform sampler2D albedo : source_color;` indexed with the hint text silently dropped from its signature. Extraction now parses the normalized copy while reading all node text from the original source — the two are byte-length- and line-identical by construction, so tree-sitter's byte ranges index both interchangeably — and a hinted or `global`/`instance` uniform's indexed signature shows the real declaration text. Ported from PR #272 by @janstol, whose independently developed `.gdshader` support crossed mid-air with #275; the surviving delta (original-source signatures, its regression fixture, and the `MORE-LANGUAGES-SUPPORT` documentation note) is landed here with attribution.
- **Ruby extraction now tracks `private`/`protected`/`public` method visibility instead of marking every symbol `Pub`.** The Ruby extractor previously hardcoded `Visibility::Pub` on every extracted node, so private helper methods showed up as public API — distorting dead-code detection, API-surface reporting, and impact analysis, all of which key off `visibility`. The extractor now tracks a per-class/module visibility mode switched by bare `private`/`protected`/`public` statements, retroactively applies the symbol-list form (`private :foo, :bar`) and the singleton-method forms `private_class_method :foo` / `public_class_method :foo`, and extracts the inline form (`private def foo; end`, previously dropped entirely since `call` nodes weren't visited). `protected` maps to `Visibility::Private` (the enum has no `Protected` variant) since both mean "not public API." Visibility mode resets at each class/module body and does not leak across sibling classes.
- **Ruby `class << self` method bodies are no longer dropped from the graph, and singleton methods are now attributed to the receiver they actually belong to.** The extractor's `visit_node` dispatch had no arm for the `singleton_class` node kind produced by `class << self` (or `class << expr`), and its fallback didn't recurse — so every method defined inside one of these blocks, an extremely common Rails idiom for declaring class methods, was silently absent: no node, no `Contains` edge, no call sites, no callers, with `dead_code`, `module_api`, `impact`, and `callers` all reporting wrong results as a consequence. `class << self` is now walked as the enclosing class's singleton scope: `def foo` inside it is indexed as `NodeKind::Method` with the same qualified name `def self.foo` would produce (no separate scope is pushed, so `Contains` still comes from the enclosing class), and it's registered alongside `def self.foo` methods so `private_class_method :foo` can retroactively target it. The block gets its own visibility scope, starting public and not leaking in either direction, matching how class/module bodies already behave — a bare `private` inside the block privatizes the class methods that follow it, and `private :foo` inside the block correctly marks the singleton rather than missing (there is no instance-method node for it to fall back to). Three follow-on receiver-attribution bugs found during review of this change are fixed in the same commit: (1) `def obj.foo` was unconditionally registered as the enclosing class's singleton regardless of receiver, so `private_class_method :foo` (or, worse, an ambiguous match against a same-named `def self.foo`) could rewrite an unrelated object's method — singleton methods are now registered only when the receiver is a literal `self` or the constant naming the enclosing class/module (`def Report.generate` inside `class Report` counts as `self`); anything else is tracked separately and excluded from *both* the instance and class-method visibility lookups, so it can't be mis-targeted by either. (2) the singleton scope used to leak into `class`/`module` bodies nested inside `class << self`, so their ordinary instance methods were wrongly registered as the enclosing class's singletons and could be hit by a singleton visibility directive; `class`/`module` traversal now resets the scope for its own body and restores it afterward, matching how `visibility_mode` and `class_depth` already behave. (3) `class << other` nested inside `class << self` used to inherit the outer scope instead of being evaluated on its own receiver; the scope is now assigned unconditionally on every `class << expr`, so a foreign nested singleton class no longer inherits an outer `self`. `class << some_object` (a non-`self` receiver) still has its body visited so methods aren't dropped, but isn't registered as a singleton of the enclosing class, since the receiver can't be resolved and doing so could let an unrelated `private_class_method` match the wrong node. Two further receiver-attribution bugs found in review of this same change are fixed alongside it: (4) a literal `self` was treated as the enclosing class unconditionally, but inside a `class << …` body `self` *is* the singleton class, so `def self.foo` written there defines a method one level further out (`Report.singleton_class`, not `Report`) — `is_enclosing_receiver`'s `"self"` arm now checks `singleton_scope == Outside`, so a `private_class_method :foo` at the `Report` level no longer reaches into a nested `class << self` and privatizes a method that was never `Report`'s; the `"constant"` arm is unaffected, since `def Report.generate` genuinely is `Report.generate` regardless of singleton scope. (5) a visibility directive written *inside* a foreign singleton body (`class << config; private :process; end`) used to exclude the foreign node correctly but then fall through to the *next* same-qualified-name match — the enclosing class's own instance method — silently flipping an unrelated public method to private; the two-bucket `want_singleton: bool` selector is replaced with a three-way `VisibilityTarget` (`Instance` / `EnclosingSingleton` / `Foreign`) so a directive can only ever mark the bucket it was actually written against. (6) `is_enclosing_receiver` only recognized `self` and a bare `constant` receiver, so a qualified constant (`class << Outer::Inner`, tree-sitter-ruby's `scope_resolution` node) always fell through to `Foreign` even when it named the exact class/module it appeared inside, leaving `private_class_method :foo` unable to reach methods defined that way — an idiom Ruby genuinely privatizes. The `"constant"` arm now also accepts `"scope_resolution"`, matched against the enclosing class/module scope chain via a new `matches_enclosing_scope_path` helper: a relative path (`Outer::Inner`, or a partial suffix like `B::C`) matches if it equals any suffix of the enclosing scopes at node-stack-entry granularity (never splitting a single scope's own name on `::`), and a leading `::` anchors the match to the *whole* scope chain, since `::B` names a different, top-level object from a same-named nested `A::B`. Known remaining gaps: a `class << self` written *inside a method body* still has its defs dropped, since only call-site extraction (not the main definition walk) descends into method bodies; and node identity is unchanged — `def self.meta_only` inside `class << self` still gets the lexical qualified name `Report::meta_only` (there's no node representing "Report's singleton class" to attach it to instead), so the fix works by having the id lists decide what a directive marks, not by giving the node a more accurate name.
- **Compact Ruby class/module definitions (`class Outer::Inner`, `module A::B`) indexed as `<anonymous>`, so every method inside one was qualified `file::<anonymous>::foo` instead of `file::Outer::Inner::foo`.** `visit_class`/`visit_module` found the name by searching `node`'s *direct* children for a `constant` node, but tree-sitter-ruby types the name field as `choice($.constant, $.scope_resolution)` — a compact definition's name is a `scope_resolution` node, which has no direct `constant` child of the class/module node itself, so the search always came up empty. Both now read the unambiguous `name` field directly instead, which covers both node kinds and, per the field's text, records the class/module's `name` as the full path (`"Outer::Inner"`) rather than just its last segment — the same convention `extract_superclass` already uses for `class Foo < Bar::Baz`. `class Foo` and `class Foo < Bar` are unaffected: the field still resolves to the same `constant` node the old child search found. This is what makes `class << Outer::Inner` reach the enclosing class in a *compact* definition: no receiver text could ever equal `<anonymous>`, so this bug was blocking the qualified-receiver fix above for the compact form specifically, even once that fix landed.

## [7.6.1] - 2026-07-24

### Added
- **Minecraft datapack `.mcfunction` indexing (#262).** Java Edition datapack function files were filtered out before extraction because no extractor claimed the extension, so a datapack project indexed zero files and grew zero call edges. A new lightweight line-based extractor (no tree-sitter grammar needed — the format is one command per line with `#` comments and no sub-file structure) now treats each `data/<namespace>/function/<path>.mcfunction` file as a function-like node named by its resource location `<namespace>:<path>` (the file *is* the function; the legacy plural `functions` directory used before Minecraft 1.21 is accepted too, and nested directories become `/`-separated path segments). Call edges are created for `function <id>`, `execute … run function <id>`, `return run function <id>`, and `schedule function <id> …` commands, including on `$`-prefixed macro lines; namespace-less targets are normalized to the implicit `minecraft:` namespace so they resolve consistently. Macro-generated targets (`foo:$(name)`) and function tags (`#ns:tag`) are preserved verbatim as unresolved references — the standard representation for dynamic targets — so they never fabricate a false edge. A leading `#` comment block becomes the function's docstring. Ships in the `full` feature tier as `lang-mcfunction`.
- **Godot `.gdshader` / `.gdshaderinc` indexing (#270).** Godot 4 shader files were likewise rejected as an unrecognized extension, leaving uniforms like `wind_strength` invisible to indexed search with no client-side workaround. Godot's shading language is GLSL-family, so instead of bundling another grammar the existing tree-sitter GLSL extractor now claims both extensions and rewrites the Godot dialect to plain GLSL before parsing: `shader_type` / `render_mode` / `group_uniforms` / `stencil_mode` file directives, `global` / `instance` uniform qualifiers, and `: hint_…` clauses on uniforms (`hint_range(…)`, `source_color`, …, up to the default value or terminator) are blanked with spaces of identical byte length, so every extracted node position still matches the original file. The result: uniforms (including hinted ones), functions and their call sites, `#include` references, `varying`s, `const`s, and structs are all indexed, and the captured `shader_type` / `render_mode` directives are re-emitted as searchable const nodes carrying their full text.
- **Verbose sync and `sync --doctor` now explain files skipped for lack of an extractor (#262, #270).** Both issues hit the same diagnostic blind spot: `sync --verbose` reported only the count of accepted files, so an unsupported language was indistinguishable from a wrong project root, an ignore rule, or a stale index. The scanner now tallies every file it rejects because no registered extractor handles its extension, and `sync --verbose` / `init --verbose` print a per-extension summary (e.g. `.mcfunction: 12 file(s) skipped (no registered extractor)`, sorted by count, capped with a roll-up line to stay readable). The tally deliberately excludes known non-source extensions — images, media, archives, lockfiles, compiled artifacts, plain-data formats like `.json`/`.yaml` — and anything already covered by exclude globs or gitignore rules, so it highlights genuinely unsupported languages instead of drowning them in `.png`/`.lock` noise. The same summary is carried on `SyncResult.skipped_extensions` and printed by `sync --doctor`, where it appears even when no files changed.
- **`tokensave memory` reports per-instance RSS for every tokensave process on the machine, to diagnose the `serve` OOM kills (#253).** Long-lived `tokensave serve` daemons have been reported reaching 8–10 GiB RSS on very large repos (~4.3M graph nodes) and triggering system OOM kills, with a follow-up sighting of four *orphaned* serve processes — parent PID 1, ~9.2 GiB each, ~20% CPU, no MCP client attached. To tell the three failure modes apart — a transient peak during sync/reference-resolution, unbounded per-tool-call growth, and abandoned processes that never exited — every tokensave process now records its own resident-set size into a machine-global, fixed-size mmap table at `~/.tokensave/memory.mmap`, and the new `tokensave memory` command renders a plain-text table across all of them. The table mirrors the `tokensave monitor` architecture: a 64-slot instance table keyed by PID, written best-effort under an `fs2` exclusive lock (any failure is silently swallowed so telemetry can never break the primary workload), reusing the `sysinfo` dependency already used for self-sampling in `runtime_telemetry`. Each slot carries PID, parent PID, start/last-update timestamps, command kind (`serve`/`sync`/`index`), project folder, current and peak RSS, the current phase, the phase during which the lifetime peak was observed (`peak_phase`), a samples counter, and the last known graph node count. Samples are taken at startup (`start`), at the MCP tool-dispatch point (attributed to the tool name, so every handler that materializes the graph is covered at one site), and bracketing the suspected transient peak in indexing/sync — `sync:resolve:load_nodes`, `sync:resolve:build_caches`, `sync:resolve:done` around the `get_all_nodes()` + `ReferenceResolver::from_nodes` step that holds ~3× the graph resident at once. The report distinguishes **alive**, **dead** (the PID is gone — its recorded `peak_rss`/`peak_phase` is the forensic record an OOM-killed process leaves behind, so dead slots stay visible), and **orphan** (a still-running process reparented to init or whose recorded parent has died — the abandoned daemons from the report). Sizes render human-readably (`9.0 GiB`); `tokensave memory --clean` purges dead slots; the command always exits 0 so incident-response scripts can poll it safely. The instrumentation adds no new dependency and is not feature-gated (matching `monitor`), so it is present in every build tier.

### Fixed
- **`tokensave_context` could miss an indexed, directly relevant symbol for a natural-language task while returning only unrelated matches for the task's generic words — even though `tokensave_search` ranked that same symbol near the top (#264).** The context builder searches each conceptual term from the task prose with a bounded per-term FTS query, but that query applied its `LIMIT` **without** BM25 ordering — a deliberate guard against repository-wide sorting that overshot: for a generic term like "icon" the handful of returned rows were effectively arbitrary (ascending rowid, i.e. indexing order), so whether a strongly matching symbol ever entered the candidate pool depended on where it happened to sit in the index rather than on how well it matched. A top-level asset generator (`generateLauncherIcon`, `generateSplashScreen`, …) could thus lose every retrieval slot to incidental UI screens that merely shared words like "icon", "screen", or "logo", and no amount of downstream re-ranking could recover a candidate that was never retrieved. The bounded per-term query now orders by the same `bm25()` weights the ranked `tokensave_search` path uses before applying its small `LIMIT` — still bounded per-term work, since only rows matching that one quoted prefix term are scored, never the whole repository. Two adjacent retrieval gaps in the same pipeline are closed alongside: the candidate pool was filled by exhausting terms in prose order, so early broad terms could consume the entire pool cap before later, more discriminating terms ("favicon", "oauth") were ever searched — terms now fetch their ranked top-K up front and fill the pool round-robin, one candidate per term per round; and because the symbol FTS tokenizer (unicode61) does not stem, a prose verb like "generates" never prefix-matched indexed names such as `generateLauncherIcon` — a trailing plural/third-person `s` on a conceptual term now also folds into a second search term ("generates" → "generate") so verb-form prose reaches the camelCase identifiers it describes.
- **`cargo test --no-default-features --features lite` compiles and passes again.** Twenty-six per-language extraction test files imported feature-gated extractors (`LeanExtractor`, `RubyExtractor`, …) without a matching `#![cfg(feature = "lang-…")]` gate, and `tests/fixture_extraction_test.rs` / `tests/project_manifest_test.rs` exercised gated languages in ungated tests, so the lite tier's test build had been failing to compile at all — meaning the tier advertised as the minimal build was never actually test-verified. Every gated-language test file now carries the matching crate-level `#![cfg]`, mixed-tier files gate the affected tests individually, and the `project_manifest` unit tests use `python` (always-compiled lite tier) instead of `bash` (medium) as their override language, since the override mechanics under test are language-agnostic.
- **`tokensave_status` no longer reports an empty, whole-history-stale graph during a full reindex while search serves current data (#267).** `index_all()` (run in the background by the MCP server after a major version upgrade or schema change) commits its up-front `Database::clear()` and then spends seconds scanning, extracting, and resolving before the bulk re-insert lands. A `tokensave_status` call landing in that committed-empty window read `node_count`/`edge_count`/`file_count`/`last_updated`/`total_source_bytes` all `0` and `files_by_language {}` from the emptied `nodes`/`edges`/`files` tables — while `db_size_bytes` (SQLite doesn't shrink the file on `DELETE`) and the `last_sync_at`/`last_full_sync_at` metadata rows (untouched by `clear()`) stayed populated, and `tokensave_search` moments later returned accurate, current results once the rebuild finished. Worse, the staleness check computed `git_commits_since(last_updated)` with `last_updated == 0` (MAX of `files.indexed_at` over an empty table), which counts *every commit in the branch history* — the reporter's "149 commit(s) since last sync" warning. `handle_status` now flags the transient state with an `index_rebuild_in_progress: true` field (detected via the live sync lock: the `.tokensave/sync.lock` file exists and records a still-alive PID) instead of presenting the empty graph as the true index state, and suppresses the staleness warning while a rebuild is in flight. When the files table is empty for any reason (an interrupted rebuild whose lock is already gone), the staleness reference timestamp falls back from the zeroed `last_updated` to the `last_sync_at` metadata value, which survives `Database::clear()`, so `git_commits_since` can no longer count the whole history. Normal-path behavior is unchanged (`last_updated > 0` keeps using it). `tokensave_status` output also now carries a `version` field (`env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION")`), a reporter side request.
- **`tokensave install --agent copilot` registered the MCP server in the wrong VS Code config file, so `doctor` permanently reported the install as missing even when Copilot had already picked the tools up (#266).** The installer wrote `mcp.servers.tokensave` into `User/settings.json`, which was VS Code's legacy/preview location for user-level MCP servers. Modern VS Code (1.102+, GA MCP) reads user-level servers from a dedicated `User/mcp.json` (top-level `servers` key) instead, and it actively migrates the old `settings.json` `mcp` key away on its own — on startup it silently moves any such entry into its internal MCP registry (which is what actually makes the server available to Copilot), and strips the key back out of `settings.json` once done. So the install could well have worked from VS Code's point of view; the real bug was that `doctor`/`has_tokensave` only ever inspected `settings.json`, and kept reporting the registration as permanently absent once VS Code had emptied out the very key they were checking — an unresolvable false-negative loop indistinguishable, from the outside, from a genuinely broken install. `install` now writes only `User/mcp.json` (and the VS Code Insiders equivalent, when present) and never touches `settings.json`, leaving any hand-maintained settings alone. `doctor`/`has_tokensave` check `mcp.json` first and fall back to a legacy `settings.json` entry for back-compat with pre-#266 installs, so either location passes; if both are registered, `doctor` now warns about the stale duplicate instead of silently ignoring it. `uninstall` cleans up both locations, so a leftover legacy registration doesn't linger after removal. `mcp.json` is now parsed as JSONC rather than strict JSON — like `settings.json`, VS Code allows comments and trailing commas in it, and treating those as a parse failure previously made `install` error out, and `doctor`/`has_tokensave`/`uninstall` silently treat the file as empty.
- **`config.toml` preferences (e.g. `wildcard_permissions`) silently reverted after being set by a concurrent process.** `UserConfig::save()` wrote its entire in-memory snapshot of `config.toml` as a whole-file overwrite, so a long-lived process (the `serve` MCP daemon, a git `post-commit`/`post-checkout` hook running `sync`) that had loaded the file before another process changed a preference — a manual edit, or `tokensave install --wildcard-permissions` — would revert that change back to the stale value on its next routine save. `save()` now re-reads the current on-disk `config.toml` and only overwrites the fields this process actually changed since its own `load()`, preserving whatever another process wrote for every field left untouched; the per-field baseline advances after every successful save instead of staying pinned to `load()` time, so a long-lived process that changes a field once doesn't keep treating it as perpetually modified and re-writing that stale value over later external changes to it. The on-disk read-modify-write span, in both `save()`'s merge and `load()`'s own config.toml read, is held under a new cross-process advisory lock (`config.toml.lock`, via the existing `fs2` dependency), since the in-process `SAVE_LOCK` alone doesn't order writes from a second *process*: without it, two concurrent processes could each merge against the same stale snapshot and lose whichever field the other had just changed, and `load()` could move aside a config.toml another process had just concurrently made valid, mistaking it for the corrupt content it had raced past. An unparseable or invalid-UTF-8 `config.toml`/`state.toml` is now also moved aside to a `config.toml.corrupt.<timestamp>.<pid>.<n>` / `state.toml.corrupt.<timestamp>.<pid>.<n>` backup on load instead of silently resetting every preference to defaults; this preservation resolves through symlinks first, so a dotfiles-managed `config.toml -> ~/dotfiles/config.toml` symlink survives (left dangling, ready for the next save to write through it again) instead of being detached from its target.

## [7.6.0] - 2026-07-22

### Changed
- **Claude Code and OpenCode rules now live in a tokensave-managed file instead of being appended to the user's own instructions (#256).** `tokensave install` previously appended a ~40-line "prefer tokensave tools" block directly into `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` (Claude) and `~/.config/opencode/AGENTS.md` (OpenCode), guarded by a marker check that skipped the append once present — so the block never refreshed on upgrade even as the rule text improved, and uninstall had to locate and cut the block back out of a file the user otherwise hand-maintains. Rules now live in a dedicated file tokensave owns outright and rewrites unconditionally on every install: `~/.claude/rules/tokensave.md` for Claude (global) / `<project>/.claude/rules/tokensave.md` (local), auto-loaded by Claude Code's existing `rules/*.md` convention with no CLAUDE.md edit needed; and a colocated `tokensave.md` for OpenCode (global: `~/.config/opencode/tokensave.md`, local: `<project>/tokensave.md`), wired in via a deduped entry in `opencode.json`'s `"instructions"` array. CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md are no longer touched by a fresh install. Installing over a pre-#256 setup migrates automatically: the old inline block is stripped from CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md and the managed file takes its place, so upgraders end up with the rules in exactly one location. `tokensave doctor` now checks for the managed file (and, for OpenCode, the `instructions` entry) instead of scanning CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md for the old marker text. Managed-file writes and the CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md migration step are atomic (temp-file + rename, backed up first) so a failed write can't truncate existing rules, migration failures now fail the install instead of being silently swallowed, and uninstall preserves a symlinked managed file (removing its real target) instead of deleting the symlink and orphaning the target — the same symlink-safety guarantee also covers the CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md migration step's empty-file cleanup, so a symlinked legacy instructions file survives migration too. Global OpenCode installs now wire `tokensave.md` into `opencode.json`'s `"instructions"` array by absolute path rather than a bare filename — OpenCode resolves a relative entry by globbing up from the current *project's* working directory, not from `opencode.json`'s own directory, so a bare `"tokensave.md"` there would never actually resolve to `~/.config/opencode/tokensave.md`. Local (project-scoped) installs keep the relative filename, which resolves correctly and stays portable across checkouts.

### Fixed
- **`tokensave_context`'s tool description no longer embeds the live graph node count, which was poisoning MCP client prompt caches (#260).** The description carried a `CALL BUDGET: N calls maximum for this project (NNNNNN nodes)` line, and the node count changes on nearly every re-index. Because MCP tool descriptions are part of the JSON schema that clients splice into their system prompt, any client that fingerprints or prompt-caches that prefix (Anthropic/OpenAI prompt caching, or an agent keeping a hashed system-prompt snapshot) saw the bytes change on almost every launch and was forced to discard and rebuild the cache — silently losing cache reads and inflating token cost and latency, even though nothing meaningful had changed. The volatile parenthetical is gone; the description is now stable across re-indexes and carries only the call-budget guidance, whose value moves at most when the project crosses a size tier. The node count is unchanged where it belongs — in `tokensave_status`' results — so no information is lost, only relocated off the cached surface.
- **`tokensave_search` in literal mode now honors `path_include` / `path_exclude` (#258).** Literal (exact-substring) search branched to its own scan before the path filters were ever parsed, so a caller pinning the search to a specific file with `path_include` still got matches for the same literal from unrelated files across the project — contradicting the tool schema, which advertises those filters on every search. Literal mode now applies the identical substring filtering the ranked-search path uses, with `path_exclude` taking precedence over `path_include`, and continues to respect the scope prefix.
- **Incremental sync no longer serves a stale view of a file edited within the same second at an unchanged byte size (#259).** The change detector skipped re-hashing any file whose stored `(mtime, size)` matched the filesystem, and `mtime` was recorded at whole-second resolution. An edit that landed in the same wall-clock second as the last index and preserved the file's byte count — common when a build tool or editor rewrites an HTML file's embedded script structure in place — was therefore indistinguishable from no change, and the old indexed symbols were served indefinitely (a full `sync --force` was the only recovery). File mtime is now captured at nanosecond resolution, shrinking that blind window to effectively zero. The value feeds only the sync pre-filter's `modified_at`, which is compared against itself across syncs, so the unit change is self-contained: databases written by an older build carry second-granular values that differ from the new nanosecond reading, triggering exactly one re-hash per file on the first sync after upgrade, after which the content-hash check confirms nothing changed and stores the refined mtime.

## [7.5.0] - 2026-07-21

### Changed
- **Split `~/.tokensave/config.toml` into stable config + machine-local state.** Volatile, frequently-changing fields (`pending_upload`, `cached_latest_version`, `last_*` timestamps, `cached_country_flags`, `installed_agents`, and similar) now persist to a new `~/.tokensave/state.toml` instead of `config.toml`, so `config.toml` only holds stable preferences (`upload_enabled`, `watcher_debounce`, `extraction_timeout_secs`, `wildcard_permissions`) and stays safe to check into a dotfiles repo without constant diffs. Existing installs migrate transparently on first load: legacy state values still in `config.toml` are read once and written out to `state.toml` on the next save, with no data loss. Both files are written atomically (temp file + rename, with a unique name per *call* so racing saves within one process — e.g. background MCP work and a shutdown save — can't collide on the same temp path; the remove-then-retry fallback for Windows' non-replacing `rename` is scoped to that exact `AlreadyExists` case rather than any rename failure) and resolve symlinks — including chains of them that dangle partway through (e.g. `config.toml -> managed-config -> missing-target` where `missing-target` doesn't exist yet, as with a freshly cloned but still-empty dotfiles repo), walked hop-by-hop with a bounded hop count so a cyclic chain (`config.toml -> other -> config.toml`) can't hang resolution and, since every hop in an exhausted chain is then known to still be a live symlink with no safe target to write through, fails the save outright rather than renaming over one of the links in the cycle — before writing, so a dotfile setup that symlinks `config.toml` into a repo gets the write through the link instead of the link being replaced by a plain file. On Windows, where `rename` doesn't replace an existing destination, the fallback now moves the existing file aside to a backup path and restores it if the replacement rename then fails (disk full, permissions, an antivirus lock, another process), instead of unconditionally deleting it first and risking a permanent loss of `state.toml`'s `pending_upload` and installed-agent bookkeeping. `state.toml` is written before `config.toml` so a failed state write can never strip legacy state out of `config.toml` without it landing in `state.toml`. An unparseable `state.toml` (a manual edit gone wrong, filesystem corruption, a future incompatible format) is moved aside to a `state.toml.corrupt.<timestamp>.<pid>.<n>` backup on load rather than being silently discarded and then overwritten in place by the very next routine save — the original content survives on disk for manual recovery, and the next save writes a fresh `state.toml` at the normal path. Within a process, the state-then-config write pair is now serialized under a mutex so two same-process savers (e.g. background MCP work racing a shutdown save) can't interleave their writes into a `config.toml` from one caller paired with a `state.toml` from the other; this does not extend across processes, which remains best-effort as before. `load()` now takes that same mutex while reading both files, so a same-process `load()` can no longer race a `save()` and return a `config.toml` snapshot from before the save paired with a `state.toml` snapshot from after it (or vice versa) — previously the save's own internal serialization did nothing to stop a concurrent reader from tearing across it. Atomic saves (Unix only) also now preserve the destination's existing file mode instead of resetting it to the process umask default: previously `std::fs::write` created the temp file with the umask's permissions (commonly `0o644`) and the rename onto the target adopted them wholesale, silently widening a dotfiles-managed `config.toml` deliberately locked to `0o600` (and creating `state.toml` — which holds local bookkeeping like `pending_upload` and `installed_agents` — world-readable by default) on every save; the temp file's mode is now set to match the resolved target's existing mode before the rename, falling back to a restrictive `0o600` for a file that doesn't exist yet (first save, or a still-dangling symlink chain), and the save fails outright if that mode can't be set rather than renaming a file whose permissions weren't pinned down deliberately.

### Fixed
- **Agent setup banners no longer print on every `init` and `sync` (#255).** The silent reinstall-on-upgrade path in `main` — which re-runs `install` for every tracked agent so permissions, hooks, and MCP config stay in sync with a new binary — called each integration's `install` directly, and those integrations print their full interactive setup output (`✔ Added tokensave MCP server to …`, `PreToolUse hook already present, skipping`, `Setup complete. Next steps: …`). On a machine with several agents installed that meant dozens of lines of install chatter in front of the actual output of an ordinary `tokensave init`/`tokensave sync`. Worse, the version markers (`last_installed_version`, `previous_version`) only advanced when *every* tracked agent's install succeeded, so a single config path that can never be written — an app that isn't installed, a read-only or permission-denied location such as a managed VS Code `settings.json` — pinned the markers permanently and re-triggered the whole resync, banner output included, on every subsequent command rather than once after an upgrade. That is why the report saw the install screen *every* time, not just after upgrading. Agent progress output now routes through `agent_note!`/`agent_note_inline!` macros gated on a process-global quiet flag, which the resync sets for its duration, so background maintenance is silent while an explicit `tokensave install` still prints exactly as before; and the markers advance regardless of per-agent failures, with the failures collected and surfaced once as a single `warning: could not refresh tokensave config for: <agents>` line pointing at `tokensave install` for the underlying error, instead of being retried forever in silence. The loop moved out of `main` into `agents::resync_installed_agents`, which takes the per-agent installer as a closure and returns which agents failed, so the marker-advance and no-repeat behavior are covered by tests that don't touch real agent config files.
- **Bash grep guardrail sees through a leading env assignment or `cd … &&` prefix.** `extract_grep_invocation` only recognized a code search when the command literally started with `grep`/`rg`/`ag` (after `rtk`/`sudo`/`time`/`nice`), so `FOO=bar grep -n Sym src/main.rs` and `cd src && grep -n Sym main.rs` silently bypassed the redirect. Leading `NAME=value` assignments and a leading `cd … &&`/`cd … ;` prefix are now stripped before the tool check. A pipeline (`… | grep`) still passes through, since piping another command's output through grep is not a code search. The opt-out is preserved and made more forgiving: an explicit inline `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK=<truthy>` (for example `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK=1 grep …`) is now honored as a deliberate bypass, exactly like the exported variable, rather than being stripped as ordinary noise and then blocked. Truthiness matches `HookEnv::from_runtime` (set, non-empty, not `0`, not `false` case-insensitive), so a falsey value such as `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK=0` is not an opt-out and the grep is still redirected.

### Added
- **Factory Droid's built-in `explorer` subagent now redirects to `tokensave_context`.** The installed `PreToolUse` matcher includes `Task`, whose live payload identifies the built-in research agent as exact lowercase `tool_input.subagent_type: "explorer"`. Indexed projects block that exact type with Droid's exit-code-2/stderr contract and point the caller to `tokensave_context`; `worker`, `implementer`, custom explorer-named types, and every other typed subagent continue unchanged. The shared `TOKENSAVE_DISABLE_GREP_HOOK` escape hatch and non-indexed fail-open behavior are preserved. Reinstalling migrates both older `Execute`-only and `^(Execute|Grep)$` entries in place to `^(Execute|Grep|Task)$` without duplicates.
- **Factory Droid guardrail hook now also covers the native `Grep` tool (follow-up to the Droid `PreToolUse` hook).** The install matcher widens from `Execute` to `^(Execute|Grep)$` (an anchored regex, verified live to still fire on both tools, so it can never collide with a future tool name that merely contains `Execute`/`Grep`), so a symbol-shaped `pattern` on a code target routed through Droid's own `Grep` tool (previously invisible to the hook) redirects to `tokensave_search`/`tokensave_callers_for`, reaching parity with the Claude hook's Grep coverage. The shared decision core now also reads Droid's `glob_pattern` field alongside Claude's `glob`, and redirects only an explicit `output_mode: "content"`; omitted, malformed, unknown, and cheap path-only modes (including Droid's `file_paths`) pass through. Re-installing over an older `Execute`-only entry migrates it in place instead of adding a duplicate, `doctor` flags stale matchers instead of reporting them healthy, and install/uninstall preserve lookalike custom wrappers by requiring an exact tokensave command. `Read`/`LS`/`Glob` are deliberately not matched because Droid's hard-block contract has no soft-steer channel for their lossy or unclassifiable equivalents; see the PR for the full per-tool analysis.

## [7.4.2] - 2026-07-19

### Changed
